$\le\kappa\cdot2^{\aleph_0}\le\kappa^{\aleph_0}\le\kappa^{<\kappa}$ for finite $\nu$ (as $\kappa>\aleph_0$). Summing over
the $\le\kappa$ values of $\nu$ gives at most $\kappa^{<\kappa}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The exact threshold of $A^{06}_\kappa$ \src{06} \merge]\label{osq:thm:model06}
Let $\kappa$ be uncountable and regular. Every homomorphism $A^{06}_\kappa\to S$ with
$|S|<\kappa^{<\kappa}$ factors through $\ct$, every $A^{06}_\kappa$-module $M$ with $|M|<\kappa^{<\kappa}$
satisfies $\Pi^{06}_\kappa M=0$, and for each $0\ne x\in\Pi^{06}_\kappa$ the least cardinality of a ring
target detecting $x$, and of a module on which $x$ acts nontrivially, is exactly $\kappa^{<\kappa}$. The
same holds for $A^{06}_\kappa[i]$ with $\Z[i]$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
\cref{osq:thm:thresholds} with \cref{osq:lem:kappagap,osq:lem:card06}: each $0\ne x\in\Pi^{06}_\kappa$ has a
gap $\epsilon_\beta<\supp x$, and with $c=\epsilon_\beta/2$ the field $L_c$ has cardinality at least
$\kappa^{<\kappa}=|A^{06}_\kappa|$.
\end{proof}

06 proves the lower bound $\kappa$ (with the explicit telescope family of the $\kappa$ monomials
$X^{a+\epsilon_\alpha}$, each witness having countable support) and, under the extra hypothesis
$\kappa^{<\kappa}=\kappa$, that $|A^{06}_\kappa|=\kappa$ and the threshold is exactly $\kappa$; that is
the special case of the theorem where $\kappa^{<\kappa}=\kappa$. The lower bounds of 06 need no cardinal
arithmetic, and neither does the exact value $\kappa^{<\kappa}$, which comes from 06's own collision lemma
(compare 03's field bound). This answers the first half of 06's question ``Cardinal arithmetic without the
matching hypothesis'': the least size of a target detecting a fixed nonzero $x\in\Pi^{06}_\kappa$ is
$\kappa^{<\kappa}$ in ZFC. The second half (``a different integer-part construction of size exactly
$\kappa$ with the same lower-bound property under weaker hypotheses'') is answered by 08 in ZFC for every
infinite $\kappa$ (\cref{osq:thm:model08}), for a ring of size exactly $\kappa$ whose fraction field,
unlike $\Frac A^{06}_\kappa=K^{06}_\kappa$, is not claimed to be its ambient field.

\begin{example}[The first uncountable cardinal \src{06}]\label{osq:ex:CH}
Under CH, $\aleph_1^{<\aleph_1}=\aleph_1^{\aleph_0}=2^{\aleph_0}=\aleph_1$, so $A^{06}_{\aleph_1}$ has
size $\aleph_1$: every action on a countable abelian group is an ordinary integer action, while the regular
module of size $\aleph_1$ sees all purely infinite elements. Without CH the lower bound at $\aleph_1$ holds,
the ring has size $\aleph_1^{\aleph_0}=2^{\aleph_0}$, and by \cref{osq:thm:model06} the exact threshold is
$2^{\aleph_0}$; this ring is isomorphic to $A^{03}_{\aleph_1}$ (\cref{osq:rem:reconcile}).
\end{example}

\subsection{A controlled field of size exactly \texorpdfstring{$\kappa$}{kappa} \texorpdfstring{\src{08}}{[08]}}\label{osq:sub:model08}

Let $\kappa$ be any infinite cardinal and $\Gamma^{08}_\kappa=\bigoplus_{\xi<\kappa}\Q e_\xi$, ordered
lexicographically with the least nonzero index deciding the sign, so $0<ne_\eta<e_\xi$ for $\xi<\eta$;
it is divisible of cardinality $\kappa$, and each nonzero element has a finite coordinate support.

\begin{lemma}[Scale and support bounds \src{08}]\label{osq:lem:gammabounds}
For every $c>0$ there are $\kappa$ positive $a\ll c$ (namely $e_\xi$ for $\xi$ beyond the least nonzero
index of $c$). If $S\subseteq(\Gamma^{08}_\kappa)_{>0}$ has fewer than $\cf\kappa$ elements, some
$\beta<\kappa$ has $e_\beta\ll s$ for all $s\in S$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
A union of fewer than $\cf\kappa$ finite subsets of $\kappa$ is bounded; take $\beta$ above it.
\end{proof}

Let $K=\R\cap\overline\Q$, $H=\hahn K{\Gamma^{08}_\kappa}$ and $H_{\mathrm{ctbl}}\subseteq H$ the series
with at most countable support. $H_{\mathrm{ctbl}}$ is a real closed subfield: finitely many series lie in
$\hahn K\Delta$ for the countable divisible subgroup $\Delta$ generated by their supports, which is real
closed \cite[Fact~2.1.1]{LM} and has only countable supports. A \emph{truncation} of a series is its
restriction to an initial segment of its support in decreasing order; a countable support has countably
many. Let $F_0$ be the real closure in $H_{\mathrm{ctbl}}$ of the field generated by $K$ and all
$X^g$; given $F_n$, adjoin all truncations of its elements and let $F_{n+1}$ be the real closure in
$H_{\mathrm{ctbl}}$ of the resulting field; put $F=\bigcup_nF_n$.

\begin{lemma}[The controlled field \src{08}]\label{osq:lem:controlled}
$F$ is real closed and truncation-closed, has cardinality $\kappa$, consists of series with countable
support, and contains $K$ and every monomial.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
An algebraic extension of an infinite field of size $\kappa$ has size $\le\kappa$ ($\kappa$ polynomials,
finitely many roots each), and adjoining countably many truncations of each of $\kappa$ elements adds at
most $\kappa$ elements; so every $F_n$, and $F$, has size $\kappa$, and at least $\kappa$ because of the
monomials. Truncations of an element of $F_n$ lie in $F_{n+1}$, and finitely many coefficients lie in a
common real closed stage.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Finite-coordinate invariant \src{08}]\label{osq:lem:finitecoord}
Every $f\in F$ has $\supp f\subseteq\Gamma_E=\bigoplus_{\xi\in E}\Q e_\xi$ for some finite $E\subseteq\kappa$
(its support need not be finite).
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
For finite $E$, $H_E=\hahn K{\Gamma_E}$ is real closed and lies in $H_{\mathrm{ctbl}}$ ($\Gamma_E$ is
countable); the $H_E$ form a directed family. An element of the field generated by the monomials is a
rational expression in finitely many of them, so lies in some $H_E$. An element of $F_0$ is algebraic over
that field, hence over some $H_E$ (finitely many coefficients); as $H_E$ is real closed and
$H_{\mathrm{ctbl}}$ is an ordered field containing it, it has no proper ordered algebraic extension there,
so the element lies in $H_E$. Truncations of elements of $H_E$ stay in $H_E$, finite rational expressions
stay in $H_{E\cup E'}$, and real closure is handled by the same algebraicity argument; induct over $n$.
\end{proof}

This invariant removes regularity: even at a singular $\kappa$ of countable cofinality, the coordinates in
one element are confined to a finite set. The proof uses countable supports to control the number of
truncations and finite coordinate sets to find smaller positive scales (08).

The map $\iota(\sum q_\xi e_\xi)=\sum q_\xi\omega^{-\xi}$ is an increasing additive embedding of
$\Gamma^{08}_\kappa$ into $\No$ (the least nonzero coordinate decides the sign on both sides), and
$\Phi(\sum a_gX^g)=\sum a_g\omega^{\iota(g)}$ is an ordered field embedding $H\hookrightarrow\No$
preserving truncations and constant terms. Put $F^{08}_\kappa=\Phi(F)$,
$A^{08}_\kappa=F^{08}_\kappa\cap\Oz=\{f\in F:\supp f\ge0,\ \ct(f)\in\Z\}$ and
$\Pi^{08}_\kappa=\ker(\ct|_{A^{08}_\kappa})$. Since $\iota(e_0)=1$, the monomial $X^{e_0}$ is the actual
surreal $\omega$. $A^{08}_\kappa$ is a discretely ordered integer part of $F$ of cardinality $\kappa$:
truncate $x$ to its positive part $P\in F$ and apply the floor rule of \cref{osq:sec:model03} to
$x=P+r+\epsilon$ with $r\in K$.

\begin{theorem}[Sharp models at every infinite cardinal \src{08}]\label{osq:thm:model08}
For every infinite cardinal $\kappa$:
\begin{enumerate}
\item $F^{08}_\kappa$ is a truncation-closed real closed subfield of $\No$ with countable supports,
$|F^{08}_\kappa|=|A^{08}_\kappa|=\kappa$, and $A^{08}_\kappa$ is an integer part of it;
\item every homomorphism from $A^{08}_\kappa$ to a ring of cardinality $<\kappa$ factors through $\ct$;
\item every quotient ring of $A^{08}_\kappa$ is a quotient $\Z/n\Z$ ($n\ge0$) under $\ct$ or has cardinality
exactly $\kappa$;
\item $\Pi^{08}_\kappa$ needs exactly $\cf\kappa$ generators;
\item every unital $A^{08}_\kappa$-module of cardinality $<\kappa$ has action $f\cdot m=\ct(f)m$;
\item the positive-characteristic maximal residue fields are the $\F_p$, with maximal ideals $pA^{08}_\kappa$;
characteristic-zero maximal residue fields exist and all have cardinality exactly $\kappa$.
\end{enumerate}
No hypothesis such as CH, GCH, $\kappa^{\aleph_0}=\kappa$ or an
inaccessible cardinal is used.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
(i) is \cref{osq:lem:controlled} and the paragraph above. Each $f\in\Pi^{08}_\kappa$ has support in some
$\Gamma_E$ (\cref{osq:lem:finitecoord}), so $e_\beta$, $\beta>\max E$, is a positive strict lower bound; and
\cref{osq:lem:gammabounds} gives $\kappa$ scales below every $c$. These are the hypotheses of
\cref{osq:thm:criterion}, which gives (ii).
(iii) If $|A/J|<\kappa$, (ii) applied to $A\to A/J$ gives $\Pi\subseteq J$; otherwise $|A/J|\le|A|=\kappa$.
(iv) With a cofinal $C\subseteq\kappa$ of size $\cf\kappa$, for $f\in\Pi^{08}_\kappa$ choose $\beta\in C$ above
its coordinate set; then $X^{-e_\beta}f\in\Pi^{08}_\kappa$, so $\{X^{e_\beta}:\beta\in C\}$ generates.
Fewer than $\cf\kappa$ elements have coordinate sets bounded by some $\beta$, so they lie in $(X^{e_\beta})$,
which misses $X^{e_\beta/2}$. (v) is \cref{osq:lem:cyclic} with (ii). (vi) Coefficient division gives
$n\Pi^{08}_\kappa=\Pi^{08}_\kappa$, so $nA=n\Z+\Pi^{08}_\kappa$ and $A/pA\cong\F_p$ is already a field; a maximal
ideal of characteristic $p$ contains $pA$ and equals it. The element $u=1+X^{e_0}$ is a nonunit
(\cref{osq:lem:units}), so the set-sized ring $A$ has a maximal ideal $M\ni u$ (ordinary maximal-ideal
theorem); if a nonzero integer $n$ lay in $M$, then $X^{e_0}\in n\Pi^{08}_\kappa\subseteq M$ and $1\in M$. A
characteristic-zero residue field of size $<\kappa$ would receive a map through $\Z$ by (ii), with image $\Z$,
not a field; and it has size $\le\kappa$ as a quotient.
\end{proof}

At $\kappa=\aleph_0$ the theorem restricts only finite targets, and the characteristic-zero residue fields are
countable and detect infinite monomials. For regular $\kappa$ the generator count is $\kappa$; at a singular
$\kappa$ it is smaller, so a countably generated ideal can be invisible to all ring targets below a much larger
singular $\kappa$ of countable cofinality. Two resources are separated: monomial generators coinitial in the
support scales ($\cf\kappa$) and pairwise collision witnesses below a fixed scale ($\kappa$) (08).

In the residue field of a maximal ideal containing $1+\omega$ the image of $\omega$ is $-1$, not $0$, and that
field has size $\kappa$; so for every infinite $\kappa$ the subring $A^{08}_\kappa\subseteq\Oz$ has a
characteristic-zero field quotient of cardinality $\kappa$ in which $\omega\mapsto-1$. This quotient map cannot
extend to $\Oz$, where every set-sized image sends $\omega$ to $0$: an explicit extension obstruction between a
set-sized integer part and the class ring (08). 08 does not identify $\Frac(A^{08}_\kappa)$ with
$F^{08}_\kappa$, imposes no initial-segment condition or birthday cutoff on the embedding, and designs the
construction for truncation, support control and cardinality, not for closure under a derivation or an
exponential; the isomorphism types of the residue fields are not classified.

\subsection{Countable supports over a long one-armed group \texorpdfstring{\src{09}}{[09]}}\label{osq:sub:model09}

Fix a cardinal $\lambda$ with $\lambda^{\aleph_0}=\lambda$; then automatically $\lambda\ge2^{\aleph_0}$,
since $\lambda^{\aleph_0}\ge2^{\aleph_0}$ (09 states $\lambda\ge2^{\aleph_0}$ as a separate hypothesis; it is
redundant). For example $\lambda=2^\nu$ for any infinite $\nu$. Neither regularity nor CH is assumed. Let
$L_\lambda=\omega_1\times\lambda$ with the lexicographic order and
$\Gamma^{09}_\lambda=\bigoplus_{\ell\in L_\lambda}\Q e_\ell$, the least nonzero index deciding the sign.

\begin{lemma}[A large small-exponent tail \src{09}]\label{osq:lem:tail}
For every countable $S\subseteq(\Gamma^{09}_\lambda)_{>0}$ there is $\alpha_0<\omega_1$ with
$e_{(\alpha,\beta)}\in U_{\Gamma}(S)$ for all $\alpha_0\le\alpha<\omega_1$ and $\beta<\lambda$; in particular
$U_\Gamma(S)$ contains $\lambda$ elements, and every countable positive set has a positive strict lower bound.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The countable union of the finite index supports has first coordinates bounded below some $\alpha_0<\omega_1$;
each $s\in S$ has its least index before every $(\alpha,\beta)$ with $\alpha\ge\alpha_0$, with positive
coefficient there.
\end{proof}

For $k\in\{\R,\C\}$ and unital $D\subseteq k$ let $H_{\lambda,k}$ be the countable-support Hahn field over
$\Gamma^{09}_\lambda$, $\Pi^{09}_{\lambda,k}$ its positive-support part and
$A^{09}_{\lambda,D}=D+\Pi^{09}_{\lambda,k}$. It is a domain with units $D^\times$, the quotients of
\cref{osq:lem:division} of its elements stay in it (countable supports), and $|A^{09}_{\lambda,D}|=\lambda$:
there are at most $\lambda^{\aleph_0}=\lambda$ countable supports and $|k|^{\aleph_0}=2^{\aleph_0}\le\lambda$
coefficient choices, and $\lambda$ positive basis monomials. With $j(\alpha,\beta)=\lambda\cdot\alpha+\beta$
(ordinal operations), $\iota(\sum q_\ell e_\ell)=\sum q_\ell\omega^{-j(\ell)}$ is an ordered embedding and
$\sum c_\gamma X^\gamma\mapsto\sum c_\gamma\omega^{\iota(\gamma)}$ embeds $H_{\lambda,\R}$ in $\No$ and
$H_{\lambda,\C}$ in $\SC$, sending $A^{09}_{\lambda,\Z}$ into $\Oz$ and $A^{09}_{\lambda,\Z[i]}$ into $\GOz$;
here $X^{e_\ell}\mapsto\omega^{\omega^{-j(\ell)}}$, the group coordinates describing exponents.

\begin{theorem}[Cardinal barrier \src{09}]\label{osq:thm:barrier}
Let $A=A^{09}_{\lambda,D}$ and $\Pi_A=\Pi^{09}_{\lambda,k}$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item If $\varphi:A\to B$ is a unital ring map with $\varphi(\Pi_A)\ne0$, then $|\varphi(A)|\ge\lambda$.
\item If $M$ is an $A$-module with $\Pi_AM\ne0$, then $|M|\ge\lambda$.
\item All maps to rings of size $<\lambda$, and all modules of size $<\lambda$, factor through $\ct$.
\item For every ideal $J$, either $\Pi_A\subseteq J$ and $A/J\cong D/\ct(J)$, or $|A/J|=\lambda$.
\end{enumerate}
In particular, in ZFC there is an explicit $A\subseteq\Oz$ of cardinality $2^{\aleph_0}$ (take
$\lambda=2^{\aleph_0}$, $D=\Z$) whose maps to countable rings factor through $\ct$, whose quotients are finite,
countably infinite, or of cardinality $2^{\aleph_0}$ (the last for every quotient not induced from $\Z$), and
whose countable modules are abelian groups with constant-term action.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
For $f\in\Pi_A$ detected by $\varphi$ or by a vector, $\supp f$ is countable, \cref{osq:lem:tail} supplies
$\lambda$ small exponents, and \cref{osq:lem:separation} with \cref{osq:lem:division} injects them into
$\varphi(A)$ (with the module structure given by $\varphi$ and the vector $1$) or into $M$. (iii) follows,
and (iv) as in \cref{osq:thm:thresholds}.
\end{proof}

When the continuum exceeds $\aleph_1$ the last statement also excludes all intermediate quotient cardinalities;
this is conditional only on that cardinal inequality (09).

\begin{lemma}[Recovering the coefficient field \src{09}]\label{osq:lem:coefffield}
Let $\varphi:A\to F$ be a unital map to a field with $\varphi(\Pi_A)\ne0$. There is a unique embedding
$\sigma:k\hookrightarrow F$ with $\varphi(rg)=\sigma(r)\varphi(g)$ for $r\in k$, $g\in\Pi_A$, and
$\sigma|_D=\varphi|_D$; in particular $F$ has characteristic $0$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Take $f\in\Pi_A$ with $\varphi(f)\ne0$ and $\sigma(r)=\varphi(rf)/\varphi(f)$ (note $rf\in\Pi_A$ even if
$r\notin A$). It is additive and unital, $(rf)(sf)=(rsf)f$ gives multiplicativity, and it is injective as a
map of fields. The identity $(rg)f=(rf)g$ gives the displayed formula and independence of $f$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Residue fields at the threshold \src{09}]\label{osq:thm:residue}
Let $A=A^{09}_{\lambda,D}$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item For every $0\ne f\in\Pi_A$ there is a maximal ideal $\mathfrak m$ with $f\equiv1\pmod{\mathfrak m}$ and
$|A/\mathfrak m|=\lambda$.
\item Every maximal ideal not containing $\Pi_A$ has a residue field of cardinality $\lambda$ with a canonical
copy of $k$.
\item For $D=\Z$, the maximal ideals containing $\Pi_A$ are the $pA$, with residue fields $\F_p$, and every other
maximal ideal has a characteristic-zero residue field of cardinality $\lambda$.
\item There are at least $\lambda$ distinct maximal ideals not containing $\Pi_A$.
\end{enumerate}
Consequently the least cardinality of a field (and of a module) detecting a given $0\ne f\in\Pi_A$ is exactly
$\lambda$; and for $D=\Z$ the intersection of all finite-index ideals is $\Pi_A\ne0$ while $\Jac(A)=0$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
(i) $1-f$ is a nonconstant nonunit; a maximal ideal of the set-sized ring $A$ containing it sends $f$ to $1$, so
the residue field has size $\ge\lambda$ by \cref{osq:thm:barrier} and $\le\lambda$ as a quotient. (ii) is
\cref{osq:thm:barrier} with \cref{osq:lem:coefffield}; (iii) uses $pA=p\Z+\Pi_A$. (iv) For $\ell<\ell'$ in
$L_\lambda$ put $\gamma=e_\ell$, $a=e_{\ell'}$; then $na<\gamma$ for all $n$, and
$q=-\sum_{n\ge1}X^{\gamma-na}\in\Pi_A$ satisfies $(1-X^a)q=X^\gamma$, so
$1=(1-X^\gamma)+(1-X^a)q$: the $\lambda$ proper ideals $(1-X^{e_\ell})$ are pairwise comaximal, and maximal
ideals above them (set-sized choice) are distinct and do not contain $\Pi_A$. For the consequences, the
residue field of (i) is a field target and a module on which $f$ acts as the identity. Every finite target has
size $<\lambda$, so the finite residual is $\Pi_A$; if $0\ne x=n+f$, a prime not dividing $n\ne0$, or (i) when
$n=0$, gives a maximal ideal avoiding $x$.
\end{proof}

All of this concerns set-sized rings and is not an application of the maximal-ideal theorem to $\Oz$. The
distinction between finite ring probes and field probes is sharp: the extra information is carried by residue
fields of the full workspace cardinality (09). The number of maximal ideals and the isomorphism types of the
residue fields are not determined; distinct maximal ideals need not give nonisomorphic fields.
$\Pi^{09}_{\lambda,k}=\bigcup_{\alpha<\omega_1}X^{e_{(\alpha,0)}}A$ is an increasing chain, and the least number of
generators of the ideal is exactly $\aleph_1$ (the chain gives $\aleph_1$ generators; a countable family lies in
$X^{2h}A$ for a small $h$ by \cref{osq:lem:tail}, which misses $X^h$); flatness is in
\cref{osq:thm:homological}.

\begin{remark}[Why the support restriction is real \src{09}]\label{osq:rem:fstar}
In the unrestricted Hahn field over $\Gamma^{09}_\lambda$ the series $f_*=\sum_{\alpha<\omega_1}X^{e_{(\alpha,0)}}$
has reverse well-ordered but uncountable support with no positive lower bound in $\Gamma^{09}_\lambda$ (a late
enough $e_{(\alpha,0)}$ lies below any given $h>0$); it is not divisible by any positive monomial within the
nonnegative-support ring and is not in $A^{09}_{\lambda,D}$. No assertion is made about the quotient
classification of that larger unrestricted ring.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Supports of size \texorpdfstring{$<\kappa$}{< kappa} over a one-armed group \texorpdfstring{\src{11}}{[11]}}\label{osq:sub:model11}

\Cref{osq:thm:coinitial} (11) applies to any divisible set group $\Gamma$ with $\coin(\Gamma_{>0})\ge\kappa$. The
coinitiality condition says that every set of fewer than $\kappa$ positive elements has a positive strict lower
bound.

\begin{proposition}[Long lexicographic groups \src{11}]\label{osq:prop:lex}
For $\kappa$ uncountable and regular and $\Gamma=\bigoplus_{\alpha<\kappa}\Q e_\alpha$ with the least nonzero
index dominant, $|\Gamma|=\kappa$ and $\coin(\Gamma_{>0})=\kappa$. The group $\Gamma^{06}_\kappa$ also has
coinitiality $\kappa$, so $A^{06}_\kappa=R^{11}_{\Gamma^{06}_\kappa,<\kappa}$ with $(D,K)=(\Z,\R)$ is an instance
of \cref{osq:thm:coinitial}.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The $e_\beta$ with $\beta$ beyond the least index of a positive vector lie below it, so $(e_\beta)_{\beta<\kappa}$
is coinitial; fewer than $\kappa$ positive vectors have boundedly many leading indices (regularity), and a later
basis vector lies below all of them. For $\Gamma^{06}_\kappa$ use \cref{osq:lem:kappagap}.
\end{proof}

11 proves sharpness under the displayed extra hypotheses $|K|\le\kappa$ and $\kappa^{<\kappa}=\kappa$: then
$|R^{11}_{\Gamma,<\kappa}|=\kappa$ and its identity map is a target of size $\kappa$ not factoring through $\ct$.
11 states explicitly that these hypotheses are assumptions for the sharpness example, not assertions about all
uncountable regular cardinals, and that regularity enters only the lexicographic example.

\begin{proposition}[Exact size and threshold \merge]\label{osq:prop:card11}
In \cref{osq:prop:lex}, for every set field $K$ and every $c>0$ the field
$L_c=\{f\in\hahn K\Gamma:|\supp f|<\kappa,\ \supp f\subseteq H_c(\Gamma)\}$ has at least $\kappa^{<\kappa}$
elements. If $|K|\le\max(\kappa,2^{\aleph_0})$ (which includes 11's hypothesis $|K|\le\kappa$), then
$|R^{11}_{\Gamma,<\kappa}|=\kappa^{<\kappa}$, and the least cardinality of a
ring target or module detecting any nonzero element of the positive-support ideal is exactly $\kappa^{<\kappa}$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Exactly as in \cref{osq:lem:card06}: the series $\sum_{\alpha\in B}X^{e_\alpha}$, $B$ a subset of size $<\kappa$
of the indices beyond the leading index of $c$, lie in $L_c$ ($e_\alpha$ decreases with $\alpha$), and the
$<\kappa$-support series form a field because inverses have support in a monoid generated by fewer than $\kappa$
elements. The upper bound counts supports of size $\nu<\kappa$ and coefficient functions: for infinite $\nu$,
$|K|^\nu\le\max(\kappa^\nu,2^\nu)=\kappa^\nu$, and for finite $\nu$,
$|K|^\nu\le\max(\kappa,2^{\aleph_0})\le\kappa^{\aleph_0}\le\kappa^{<\kappa}$; also $|D|\le|K|$, and the
support choices number $\kappa^\nu$.
The threshold follows from \cref{osq:prop:fieldbound}, the coinitiality gap, and the identity map.
\end{proof}

So the sharpness of \cref{osq:thm:coinitial} in the lexicographic example holds without the hypothesis
$\kappa^{<\kappa}=\kappa$ once the bound is stated as $\kappa^{<\kappa}$, which equals $\kappa$ exactly when
$\kappa^{<\kappa}=\kappa$; the coefficient field is still assumed to satisfy $|K|\le\max(\kappa,2^{\aleph_0})$.
With $(D,K)=(\Z,\R)$ this ring is a Hahn integer part of the kind treated in this section; 11 does not
embed it in $\No$, and no embedding is used in this section. Manuscript~17 supplies one: $e_\alpha\mapsto\omega^{-\alpha}$
embeds $\Gamma$ in $(\No,+)$ as an ordered group, and hence $R^{11}_{\Gamma,<\kappa}$ in $\Oz$ for $(D,K)=(\Z,\R)$ and in $\GOz$
for $(\Z[i],\C)$ (\cref{osq:rel:lem:embedding}).

\section{Flat ideals and homological reflection}\label{osq:sec:homological}

All rings, modules, resolutions, tensor products and $\Tor$, $\Ext$ groups in this section are
ordinary set-sized objects over the set-sized rings of \cref{osq:sec:models}. No class-sized
resolution is formed, and no directed-colimit theorem is applied to the proper-class union
$\Pi=\bigcup_{a>0}\omega^a\Oz$ (03, 06, 09). The package is 06's, proved there for $A^{06}_\kappa$;
03 and 09 prove its flatness and $\Tor$ parts for their rings. The proofs use only the following
properties, which all five constructions have:

\begin{enumerate}
\item[(H1)] $A$ is a commutative domain, $A=D\oplus I$ with $I=\ker\ct$ an ideal and $D=A/I$;
\item[(H2)] $A$ is built over a divisible exponent group $\Gamma$, and every $f\in I$ has the form
$f=X^gb$ with $g\in\Gamma_{>0}$ and $b\in I$; hence $I=\bigcup_{h\in\Gamma_{>0}}X^hA$, a directed
set-indexed union of principal ideals;
\item[(H3)] $I$ is not finitely generated.
\end{enumerate}
(H2) is the gap property used in \cref{osq:thm:thresholds} (a positive strict lower bound $g$ of
$\supp f$ gives $b=X^{-g}f\in I$). (H3) holds because the least number of generators is $\cf\kappa$,
$\kappa$, $\cf\kappa$, $\aleph_1$, respectively $\kappa$ (\cref{osq:thm:model03}(iv),
\cref{osq:prop:kappachain}, \cref{osq:thm:model08}(iv), the chain after \cref{osq:thm:residue}, and for
$R^{11}_{\Gamma,<\kappa}$ the coinitial chain $(X^{e_\alpha})_{\alpha<\kappa}$ of \cref{osq:prop:lex}:
fewer than $\kappa$ elements have a common monomial divisor $X^h$, and $X^hA$ misses $X^{h/2}$); these
counts are infinite.

\begin{lemma}[Projective ideals of a domain \src{06}]\label{osq:lem:projideal}
A nonzero projective ideal of a commutative domain is finitely generated.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Fix $0\ne a\in J$. For $A$-linear $f:J\to A$ and $x\in J$, $af(x)=f(ax)=xf(a)$, so $f(a)=0$ forces
$f=0$. A dual basis $(u_s,f_s)$ has $x=\sum_sf_s(x)u_s$ with finitely many nonzero terms for each $x$;
only finitely many $f_s(a)$ are nonzero, all other $f_s$ vanish identically, and the finitely many
corresponding $u_s$ generate $J$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Homological package \src{03, 06, 09} \merge]\label{osq:thm:homological}
Let $(A,I,D)$ satisfy (H1)--(H3). Then:
\begin{enumerate}
\item $I$ is flat, $I^2=I$ (each element is a product of two elements of $I$), and $I$ is not projective;
\item for every $D$-module $N$ (inflated along $\ct$), $I\otimes_AN=0$ and $\Tor^A_n(D,N)=0$ for
$n>0$; in particular $\Tor^A_n(D,D)=0$;
\item $D$ is not flat: $\fd_AD=1$, and $\Tor^A_1(D,A/xA)\cong D$ for every $0\ne x\in I$;
\item for all $D$-modules $N,M$ and all $n\ge0$, inflation induces natural isomorphisms
$\Ext^n_A(N,M)\cong\Ext^n_D(N,M)$;
\item $\pd_AD\ge2$;
\item the sequence $0\to I\to A\xrightarrow{\ct}D\to0$ does not split, so $\Ext^1_A(D,I)\ne0$;
\item if $(x_\alpha)_{\alpha\in\Lambda}$ generates $I$, $P=\bigoplus_{\alpha}Ae_\alpha\to I$,
$e_\alpha\mapsto x_\alpha$, and $J$ is its kernel, then $\Ext^2_A(D,J)\ne0$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
(i) Each $X^hA$ is free of rank one because $A$ is a domain, the union in (H2) is directed
($X^hA\subseteq X^{h'}A$ for $h'<h$), and a filtered colimit of flat modules is flat
\cite[Lemma~10.39.3]{StacksFlat}. The factorization $f=X^gb$ writes $f$ as a product of two elements
of $I$, so $I^2=I$. Projectivity would contradict (H3) by \cref{osq:lem:projideal}.
(ii) Since $IN=0$, $I\otimes_AN\cong(I/I^2)\otimes_DN=0$ (the map $x\otimes n\mapsto(x+I^2)\otimes n$ is
well defined in reverse because $(ab)\otimes n=b\otimes an=0$ for $a,b\in I$); tensoring the flat
resolution $0\to I\to A\to D\to0$ with $N$ computes all positive $\Tor$ groups as $0$.
(iii) The same resolution gives $\fd_AD\le1$. For $0\ne x\in I$, $0\to A\xrightarrow{x}A\to A/xA\to0$ is a
free resolution; after $\otimes_AD$ the first map is $0$ because $x\in I$, so $\Tor_1^A(D,A/xA)\cong D\ne0$
and $D$ is not flat. Equivalently, the injection $xA\hookrightarrow A$ becomes the zero map $D\to D$ after
tensoring (03, 09).
(iv) Take a projective $A$-resolution $P_\bullet\to N$. By (ii), $D\otimes_AP_\bullet$ has homology $N$ in
degree $0$ only, and its terms are projective $D$-modules (direct summands of free ones), so it is a
projective $D$-resolution; the adjunction $\Hom_A(P_\bullet,M)\cong\Hom_D(D\otimes_AP_\bullet,M)$ is an
isomorphism of complexes, and comparison of resolutions makes the result natural.
(v) If $\pd_AD\le1$, the kernel $I$ of the free surjection $A\to D$ would be projective (Schanuel), against
(i).
(vi) A section $s$ gives $r=s(1)$ with $\ct(r)=1$ and $xr=s(x\cdot1)=0$ for $0\ne x\in I$, so $r=0$ in the
domain $A$.
(vii) $P\to I$ is surjective; $0\to J\to P\to I\to0$ does not split (else $I$ would be a summand of a free
module, hence projective), giving $0\ne\Ext^1_A(I,J)$; applying $\Hom_A(-,J)$ to
$0\to I\to A\to D\to0$ with $A$ projective gives $\Ext^1_A(I,J)\cong\Ext^2_A(D,J)$, represented by the
spliced sequence $0\to J\to P\to A\to D\to0$.
\end{proof}

In standard terms, $A\to D$ is a homological ring epimorphism ($D\otimes_AD=D$, all higher self-$\Tor$
vanish) but not a flat one (03, 06); this terminology is optional. The exact value of $\pd_AD$ is not
determined in general: only $\pd_AD\ge2$ and $\fd_AD=1$ are proved (06); when $I$ is countably generated,
$\pd_AD=2$ (\cref{osq:hd:prop:pd}(v), from 12's telescope). Hypothesis (H2) is where the totally ordered exponent
cone enters: for 12's coordinatewise cones the flat dimension of $D$ is any $d\ge1$, and the two kinds of
statement are compared in \cref{osq:hd:rem:reconcile}. The nonzero $\Tor_1^A(D,A/xA)$ does not
contradict (ii), because $A/xA$ is not a $D$-module: $I\not\subseteq xA$, since a gap below $\supp x$
exhibits a smaller positive monomial of $I$ outside $xA$ (06). $I/I^2=0$ is not a finite-presentation or
smoothness statement (09).

\begin{theorem}[Exact size of cohomological witnesses \src{06} \merge]\label{osq:thm:extthreshold}
Let $A$ be one of the five rings of \cref{osq:sub:organizing}, with $D=\Z$ (or any admissible $D$), and
$\tau=|A|$. For every $A$-module $M$ with $|M|<\tau$ and every $n>0$, $\Ext^n_A(D,M)=0$, and for any two
$A$-modules of size $<\tau$ the $\Ext$ groups over $A$ are those of the underlying $D$-modules. For
$n\in\{1,2\}$,
\[
 \min\{|M|:\Ext^n_A(D,M)\ne0\}=\tau .
\]
For $A^{06}_\kappa$ this minimum is $\kappa^{<\kappa}$ in ZFC; under $\kappa^{<\kappa}=\kappa$ it is $\kappa$,
which is 06's statement. For $A^{03}_\kappa$, $A^{08}_\kappa$ and $A^{09}_{\lambda,D}$ it is $\kappa^{\aleph_0}$,
$\kappa$ and $\lambda$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Modules of size $<\tau$ are annihilated by $I$ (\cref{osq:thm:thresholds}), so they are inflated $D$-modules
and \cref{osq:thm:homological}(iv) applies; $D$ is free over itself. For the upper bounds, $|I|=\tau$ and
$\Ext^1_A(D,I)\ne0$. For $n=2$ choose a generating family of cardinality at most $\tau$ (the generator counts of (H3) are all $\le\tau$); then $|P|\le\tau$, so
$|J|\le\tau$, and $\Ext^2_A(D,J)\ne0$ with the lower bound forces $|J|=\tau$.
\end{proof}

The $\Ext$ groups are computed in the full category of $A$-modules, not only among small modules; this is why
\cref{osq:thm:homological}(iv) is an ambient derived-functor statement and not merely an equivalence of small
module categories (06). The two witnesses show that a quotient can look homologically projective against every
small coefficient module while failing projectivity in the ambient category. The whole argument works for the
Gaussian versions with $D=\Z[i]$, where $\Ext_D$ means extensions of $\Z[i]$-modules (06). Finally, for
$0\ne x\in\Pi^{06}_\kappa$ the module $A^{06}_\kappa/xA^{06}_\kappa$ is detected by $I$ (it is not a $D$-module), so
its carrier has at least $\kappa^{<\kappa}$ elements; 06 stated this with the qualifier ``under the
cardinal-arithmetic hypothesis'', which is not needed.

\section{Homological and tensor algebra of omnific cores}\label{osq:hd:sec:cores}

This section is the part contributed by manuscripts~12 and~14 (\cref{osq:sub:sources}). Both
study ordinary commutative algebra of rings of the form ``constants plus a tail'' inside $\Oz$ and
$\GOz$, and they share one set-sized model: 14's finite-support ring $A_0$ is 12's rank-one
coordinate-cone core $A_1$, and 12's two-generator relation module is the case $r=2$ of 14's
lattice syzygy. Each is printed once (\cref{osq:hd:lem:cores,osq:tn:thm:syzygy}). Manuscript~12
computes flat and projective dimensions of set-sized finite-support cores
(\cref{osq:hd:sub:cores,osq:hd:sub:resolution,osq:hd:sub:tor,osq:hd:sub:dimensions}); 14 computes
tensor products, symmetric and exterior powers, Rees algebras, homomorphisms and duals of
coefficient-lattice modules, over the full class ring and over $A_0$
(\cref{osq:tn:sub:lattices,osq:tn:sub:tensor,osq:tn:sub:rees,osq:tn:sub:hom}). Manuscript~17 of the third batch
re-derives, independently, 14's matrix, syzygy, $\Hom$, tensor-torsion and power-growth theorems and 12's intersection;
these are printed once, with 17 added to their sources, and 17's new material (presentations and relation counts, the
$\Tor$ identification, moduli, orders, the lexicographic models inside $\Oz$) is \cref{osq:rel:sub:relations}. Labels carry
the sub-prefixes \texttt{osq:hd:} (12), \texttt{osq:tn:} (14) and \texttt{osq:rel:} (17).

\paragraph{Scope.} The rings of \cref{osq:hd:sub:cores,osq:hd:sub:resolution,osq:hd:sub:tor,osq:hd:sub:dimensions}
and 14's model $A_0$ have \emph{finite} supports. They are therefore outside the universal theorem, like
the finite-support rings of \cref{osq:sec:support}: the augmentation that sends every monomial to $1$
is a unital map to the coefficient field that detects the tail (\cref{osq:hd:prop:finite}). What 12
calls arithmetic invisibility is invisibility to \emph{finite} rings and to modules on which the tail
acts by zero; these rings are not invisible to set-sized rings in the sense of \cref{osq:thm:universal}.

\paragraph{Notation.} 12's augmentation $\varepsilon$ is $\ct$; its tail $M$ is $\Pi_\Lambda$ (the letter $M$ is
14's coefficient lattice); its ordered-cone ring $T=D\oplus P$ with monomials $t^\gamma$ is
$T_\Gamma=D\oplus\Pi^{\mathrm{fin}}_\Gamma$ with monomials $X^\gamma$ (in this report $t^g$ means $X^{-g}$,
\cref{osq:sub:orientation}); its test modules $N_F$ are $V_F$; its cyclic module $Q=A/(X_1,cX_1)$ is written
$A/H$. 14's ideal $\mathcal I$, its rings $B=k+\mathcal I$, $A=D+\mathcal I$ and its field $F$ are $\Pi_k$,
$\A_{k,k}$, $\A_{D,k}$ and $\hahn k\No$; its set-sized $P$, $A_0$, $B_0$ are $\Pi^{\mathrm{fin}}_\Q$,
$T_\Q$ and $k+\Pi^{\mathrm{fin}}_\Q$; its multiplication map $\mu_{M,N}$, kernel $K(M,N)$ and carrier $E(M,N)$
are $\mathsf m_{M,N}$, $\mathcal T(M,N)$ and $\mathsf E(M,N)$; its multiplier ring $\mathcal O(M)$ and orders
$\mathcal O$ in number fields $K$ are $\mathfrak o(M)$ and $\mathfrak o\subset K_0$ (the letter $\OO$ denotes the
finite surreal rings); the saturated lattice $L$ of its matrix theorem is $W_D$. 17's positive-support ideal $\mathfrak m$
(which it warns is not the infinitesimal ideal; here $\mm$ is the negative-support ideal) is $\Pi$, its $F=\Frac(D)$ is written
$\Frac(D)$ (14's $F$ was the fraction field $\hahn k\No$), its lattices $L,M$ are $M,N$, its ideals
$J_L^\gamma=\omega^\gamma(L+\mathfrak m)$ are $J_\gamma(M)=X^\gamma L(M)$, its multiplier ring $E(L)$ and conductor $(M:L)$ are
$\mathfrak o(M)$ and $(N:M)$, its multiplication map $\mu_{L,M}$ and kernel $T(L,M)$ are $\mathsf m_{M,N}$ and $\mathcal T(M,N)$
(the letter $\mu$ was 14's generator count), its kernel dimensions $d,r$ are $q,\ell$, and its set-sized models $A_\kappa$,
$\mathfrak m_\kappa$ are $R^{11}_{\Gamma_\kappa,<\kappa}$ and $\Pi^{11}_\kappa$.

\subsection{Coordinate-cone and ordered-cone cores}\label{osq:hd:sub:cores}

Throughout \cref{osq:hd:sub:cores,osq:hd:sub:resolution,osq:hd:sub:tor}, $K$ is a field and $D\subseteq K$ a unital
subring, so $D$ is a domain. Let $\Lambda$ be $[d]=\{1,\dots,d\}$ ($d\ge1$) or $\N_{>0}$, and let
$P_\Lambda=\Q_{\ge0}^{(\Lambda)}$ be the monoid of finitely supported vectors
$\boldsymbol\alpha=(\alpha_i)_{i\in\Lambda}$ with nonnegative rational coordinates. In the monoid algebra
$K[P_\Lambda]$ (finite sums, $X^{\boldsymbol\alpha}X^{\boldsymbol\beta}=X^{\boldsymbol\alpha+\boldsymbol\beta}$) put
\begin{equation}\label{osq:hd:eq:cores}
 \Pi_\Lambda=\bigoplus_{0\ne\boldsymbol\alpha\in P_\Lambda}KX^{\boldsymbol\alpha},\qquad
 A_\Lambda=D\oplus\Pi_\Lambda ,
\end{equation}
and write $A_d,\Pi_d$ for $\Lambda=[d]$ and $X_i=X^{\mathbf e_i}$. The coefficient at $X^{\mathbf 0}$ is a split
surjective ring homomorphism $\ct:A_\Lambda\to D$ with kernel $\Pi_\Lambda$, because a sum of two nonnegative
vectors is zero only when both are. For a nonzero divisible subgroup $\Gamma\subseteq\R$ put
\begin{equation}\label{osq:hd:eq:T}
 \Pi^{\mathrm{fin}}_\Gamma=\bigoplus_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{>0}}KX^\gamma,\qquad
 T_\Gamma=D\oplus\Pi^{\mathrm{fin}}_\Gamma ,
\end{equation}
the finite-support ring of \cref{osq:sec:support} over the fixed ordered group $\Gamma$, again with
$\ct:T_\Gamma\to D$. The difference between the two families is the exponent cone: coordinatewise
nonnegative vectors in $A_\Lambda$, the positive cone of a totally ordered group in $T_\Gamma$.

\begin{lemma}[Domains and embeddings \src{12; 14}]\label{osq:hd:lem:cores}
\leavevmode
\begin{enumerate}
\item $K[P_\Lambda]$, $A_\Lambda$ and $T_\Gamma$ are domains, and $\Pi_\Lambda$ is a nonzero prime ideal.
\item Let $(\gamma_i)_{i\in\Lambda}$ be positive surreals linearly independent over $\Q$. Then
$X^{\boldsymbol\alpha}\mapsto\omega^{\sum_i\alpha_i\gamma_i}$ defines an injective ring homomorphism
$\R[P_\Lambda]\to\No$, and, applied to real and imaginary parts, $\C[P_\Lambda]\to\SC$; it maps $\Z+\Pi_\Lambda$
into $\Oz$ and $\Z[i]+\Pi_\Lambda$ into $\GOz$. The $\gamma_i$ may be taken real: $\gamma_i=\theta^{i-1}$ for a
transcendental real $\theta>1$. Likewise $X^\gamma\mapsto\omega^\gamma$ embeds $T_\Gamma$ in $\Oz$ or $\GOz$ for
$(D,K)=(\Z,\R)$ or $(\Z[i],\C)$.
\item If the $\gamma_i$ are positive reals linearly independent over $\Q$ and $\Gamma=\sum_i\Q\gamma_i$, then
$X_i\mapsto X^{\gamma_i}$ is an injective ring homomorphism $A_\Lambda\hookrightarrow T_\Gamma$ compatible with
$\ct$. For $d=1$ and $\gamma_1=1$ it is an isomorphism $A_1\cong T_\Q$; this ring, with $(D,K)=(\Z,\R)$ or
$(\Z[i],\C)$, is 14's set-sized model $A_0$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
(i) Finitely many elements of $K[P_\Lambda]$ involve finitely many coordinates and a common denominator $n$ of
their exponents, so they lie in the polynomial ring $K[X_{i_1}^{1/n},\dots,X_{i_s}^{1/n}]$, a domain. In $T_\Gamma$
the largest exponents of two nonzero finite sums add and their leading coefficients multiply. $A_\Lambda/\Pi_\Lambda=D$
is a domain and $X_1\in\Pi_\Lambda$. (ii) The map $\ell(\boldsymbol\alpha)=\sum_i\alpha_i\gamma_i$ is additive,
injective by linear independence, and positive on nonzero $\boldsymbol\alpha\in P_\Lambda$; so the monomials go to
distinct Conway monomials, $\omega^u\omega^v=\omega^{u+v}$ and finite distributivity give a ring homomorphism, and
uniqueness of finite normal forms gives injectivity. Nonconstant terms go to positive exponents and the constant
coefficient is an integer (Gaussian integer), which is membership in $\Oz$ ($\GOz$). A rational relation among
the $\theta^{i-1}$ would give a nonzero rational polynomial vanishing at $\theta$. The case of $T_\Gamma$ is the same
with $\ell=\mathrm{id}$. (iii) $\ell$ is an injective monoid map $P_\Lambda\to\Gamma_{\ge0}$ with $\ell^{-1}(0)=0$; for
$d=1$ it is $\Q_{\ge0}\to\Q_{\ge0}$, the identity.
\end{proof}

Thus $d$ counts independent monomial directions, not Archimedean scales: with $\gamma_i=\theta^{i-1}$ all exponents lie
in the single Archimedean class of $1$ (12). Every element of every ring in this subsection is a finite sum; no
topology, convergence or infinite summation is involved.

\begin{proposition}[Finite targets only \src{12}]\label{osq:hd:prop:finite}
Let $A$ be $A_\Lambda$ or $T_\Gamma$ with tail $\Pi$, and $(D,K)=(\Z,\R)$ or $(\Z[i],\C)$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item For $0\ne a\in D$, $a\Pi=\Pi$ and $A/aA\cong D/aD$. Every unital map from $A$ to a finite ring, and every
finite $A$-module, factors through $\ct$; $\widehat A\cong\widehat D$ for the profinite completions,
$\varprojlim_kA/p^kA\cong\varprojlim_kD/p^kD$ for every ordinary prime $p$, $\bigcap_kp^kA=\Pi$, and
$\Tor^A_{>0}(D,E)=0$ for every finite $A$-module $E$.
\item The augmentation $\sum_{\boldsymbol\alpha}c_{\boldsymbol\alpha}X^{\boldsymbol\alpha}\mapsto\sum_{\boldsymbol\alpha}c_{\boldsymbol\alpha}$
is a unital ring homomorphism $A\to K$ sending every monomial to $1$; it does not factor through $\ct$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
(i) Divide the coefficients of a tail by $a$; so $aA=aD\oplus\Pi$. A finite ring has a positive characteristic $n$,
so a map to it kills $n\Pi=\Pi$; a finite module is killed by some $n\ge1$, and $m=n(m/n)$ for $m\in\Pi$. The ideals
$nA=nD\oplus\Pi$ are cofinal among ideals of finite index, and $\bigcap_nnD=0=\bigcap_kp^kD$ (coordinatewise for
$\Z[i]$). A finite module is therefore a $D$-module, and the $\Tor$ assertion is \cref{osq:hd:cor:arithmeticTor} below for
$A_\Lambda$ and \cref{osq:thm:homological}(ii) with \cref{osq:hd:thm:orderedcone} for $T_\Gamma$. (ii) is the computation of
\cref{osq:thm:support}(ii): for finite supports, the coefficient sum of a product is the product of the coefficient
sums.
\end{proof}

Hence all the cores, and 14's $A_0$, have the same finite quotients and profinite completion as $D$, while
\cref{osq:hd:cor:fd} below shows that their homological dimensions differ; this limits any reconstruction of an
omnific subring from its finite arithmetic shadows (12). Item (ii) is the reason the word ``invisible'' must be read
with (i)'s restriction.

\subsection{Flat face ideals and a Boolean resolution}\label{osq:hd:sub:resolution}

For a finite nonempty $S\subseteq\Lambda$ put $p_S=\prod_{i\in S}X_i$ and let
\begin{equation}\label{osq:hd:eq:IS}
 I_S=\bigoplus_{\boldsymbol\alpha\in P_\Lambda,\ \alpha_i>0\ (i\in S)}KX^{\boldsymbol\alpha},
\end{equation}
an ideal of $A_\Lambda$ with $I_S=\bigcap_{i\in S}I_{\{i\}}$ and $\Pi_\Lambda=\sum_iI_{\{i\}}$.

\begin{lemma}[Face ideals \src{12}]\label{osq:hd:lem:faces}
Let $A=A_\Lambda$ and $\Pi=\Pi_\Lambda$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item $I_S=\bigcup_{n\ge0}p_S^{2^{-n}}A$, an increasing chain of free rank-one $A$-modules; hence $I_S$ is flat.
\item $\Pi^2=\Pi$, $I_S^2=I_S$ and $\Pi I_S=I_S$; for every $D$-module $E$, regarded as an $A$-module through $\ct$,
$I_S\otimes_AE=0$.
\item Neither $\Pi$ nor any $I_S$ is finitely generated.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
(i) Multiplication by $p_S^{2^{-n}}$ is injective (\cref{osq:hd:lem:cores}), and
$p_S^{2^{-n}}=p_S^{2^{-n-1}}p_S^{2^{-n-1}}$ gives the chain. Given $f\in I_S$, choose $n$ with $2^{-n}$ below every
coordinate indexed by $S$ of the finitely many exponents of $f$; then $f/p_S^{2^{-n}}$ has nonnegative exponents,
positive on $S$, so it lies in $\Pi\subseteq A$ although its coefficients are arbitrary in $K$. A filtered colimit of
flat modules is flat \cite[Lemma~10.39.3]{StacksFlat}.
(ii) $kX^{\boldsymbol\alpha}=(kX^{\boldsymbol\alpha/2})X^{\boldsymbol\alpha/2}$, both factors in $I_S$ when
$\boldsymbol\alpha$ is positive on $S$. Since $\Pi E=0$, $I_S\otimes_AE\cong(I_S/\Pi I_S)\otimes_DE=0$.
(iii) Let $|\boldsymbol\alpha|=\sum_i\alpha_i$. If finitely many elements generated $\Pi$ (or $I_S$), let $\delta>0$ be
the least total degree of their monomials; every element of the generated ideal has only monomials of total degree
at least $\delta$, since multiplication by $A$ adds nonnegative degrees and cancellation creates no new monomial. But
$X_1^q\in\Pi$ (respectively $p_S^q\in I_S$) with $q$ small has smaller total degree.
\end{proof}

Flatness of every $I_S$ does not imply flatness of their sum $\Pi$; the failure begins at $d=2$
(\cref{osq:hd:cor:fd}). For $p\ge1$ put $C_p=\bigoplus_{S\subseteq\Lambda,\ |S|=p}I_S$ and $C_0=A$; for
$S=\{s_1<\dots<s_p\}$ let $d_p$ send $f$ in the summand $I_S$ to $\sum_{r=1}^p(-1)^{r-1}f$ in the summands
$I_{S\setminus\{s_r\}}$ (for $p=1$ the target is $A$ and the map is the inclusion). Deleting two indices in either
order gives opposite signs, so $d_{p-1}d_p=0$.

\begin{theorem}[Coordinate-intersection resolution \src{12}]\label{osq:hd:thm:resolution}
The augmented complex
\[
 \cdots\longrightarrow C_2\xrightarrow{d_2}C_1\xrightarrow{d_1}A_\Lambda\xrightarrow{\ct}D\longrightarrow0
\]
is exact and every $C_p$ is flat. For $\Lambda=[d]$, $C_p=0$ for $p>d$, and this is a flat resolution of $D$ of
length $d$; also for $\Lambda=\N_{>0}$ the complex is exact.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
All maps preserve the exponent grading. In degree $\mathbf 0$ only $A$ and $D$ contribute, and $\ct$ is the identity
of $D$. Fix $\boldsymbol\alpha\ne\mathbf 0$ with support $T=\{i:\alpha_i>0\}$. The degree-$\boldsymbol\alpha$ part of
$I_S$ is $K$ exactly when $\varnothing\ne S\subseteq T$, so the degree-$\boldsymbol\alpha$ part of the complex is the
augmented chain complex of the full simplex on $T$ with coefficients $K$, a $p$-subset in degree $p$. With $t=\min T$,
the homotopy that inserts $t$ (with the sign placing it in the ordered list) into subsets not containing $t$, is zero on
subsets containing $t$, and sends the augmentation vector to the vertex $\{t\}$, satisfies $dh+hd=1$. Each element
involves finitely many degrees and summands, so degreewise exactness is exactness. Flatness is
\cref{osq:hd:lem:faces}(i) and closure under direct sums.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Arithmetic modules are $\Tor$-acyclic \src{12}]\label{osq:hd:cor:arithmeticTor}
For every $D$-module $E$, $D\otimes_{A_\Lambda}E\cong E$ and $\Tor^{A_\Lambda}_j(D,E)=0$ for $j>0$; in particular
$A_\Lambda\to D$ is a homological ring epimorphism.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Tensor the resolution with $E$: every positive term vanishes by \cref{osq:hd:lem:faces}(ii), and flat resolutions
compute $\Tor$.
\end{proof}

This concerns every module on which the tail acts by zero; it is why such modules cannot see the flat dimension
computed next (12).

\begin{proposition}[Formal lifting and differentials \src{12; 13}]\label{osq:hd:prop:etale}
Let $A$ be $A_\Lambda$ or $T_\Gamma$ with tail $\Pi$. Then $A\to D$ is formally \'etale for nilpotent lifting
problems, but neither flat nor finitely presented as a ring map, and $\Omega_{A/D}\otimes_AD=0$: every
$D$-derivation of $A$ into a module killed by $\Pi$ vanishes.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
If $f:A\to R$ is an $A$-algebra structure map and $D\to R/J$ an $A$-algebra map with $J^n=0$, then $f(\Pi)\subseteq J$,
so $f(\Pi)=f(\Pi^n)\subseteq J^n=0$ ($\Pi^2=\Pi$ by halving exponents) and $f$ factors uniquely through $D$; this is
the idempotent-ideal mechanism of \cite[Tag~060H]{StacksFormal}. Multiplication by a nonzero monomial of $\Pi$ is
injective on $A$ but becomes $0$ on $D=D\otimes_AA$, so $D$ is not flat; $A/\Pi$ is finitely presented only if $\Pi$ is
finitely generated, which it is not (\cref{osq:hd:lem:faces}(iii); for $T_\Gamma$, a finite family of tails has a least
exponent among its monomials). A derivation $\partial$ as stated satisfies $\partial(uv)=u\partial v+v\partial u=0$ for
$u,v\in\Pi$, and $\Pi=\Pi^2$.
\end{proof}

The mechanism is classical and not a novelty claim (12). Manuscript~13 proves the same statements for the full
omnific ring in a two-universe reading (\cref{osq:hd:rem:reconcile}), and \cref{osq:thm:derivations} is the
class-ring analogue for derivations. ``Nilpotent'' is a test condition on $R$: the cores are domains.

\subsection{The multigraded Tor and exact flat dimensions}\label{osq:hd:sub:tor}

For a finite nonempty $F\subseteq\Lambda$ let $J_F=(X_i:i\in F)A_\Lambda$ and $V_F=A_\Lambda/J_F$. For finite $S$ put
$\mathbf e_S=\sum_{i\in S}\mathbf e_i$; the notation $K\langle\mathbf e_S\rangle$ means a copy of the $D$-module $K$
placed in multidegree $\mathbf e_S$ (not a subring of $A_\Lambda$).

\begin{theorem}[Boolean-face $\Tor$ formula \src{12}]\label{osq:hd:thm:boolean}
For $\Lambda$ finite or countably infinite and $F$ as above,
\begin{align*}
 \Tor_0^{A_\Lambda}(D,V_F)&\cong D\langle\mathbf 0\rangle,\qquad
 \Tor_1^{A_\Lambda}(D,V_F)\cong\bigoplus_{i\in F}D\langle\mathbf e_i\rangle,\\
 \Tor_p^{A_\Lambda}(D,V_F)&\cong\bigoplus_{S\subseteq F,\ |S|=p}K\langle\mathbf e_S\rangle\qquad(p\ge2).
\end{align*}
In particular $\Tor_p=0$ for $p>|F|$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Tensor \cref{osq:hd:thm:resolution} with $V_F$; the positive terms become $I_S/J_FI_S$ and the degree-zero term is $V_F$.
Fix $\boldsymbol\alpha\ne\mathbf 0$ with support $T$. If $\alpha_j>1$ for some $j\in F$, then $\boldsymbol\alpha-\mathbf e_j$
is nonnegative, nonzero and positive wherever $\boldsymbol\alpha$ is, so the whole coefficient space at
$\boldsymbol\alpha$ lies in $X_jI_S$ and in $X_jA$; every term vanishes in this degree. So assume $\alpha_j\le1$ for $j\in F$
and put $U=\{j\in F:\alpha_j=1\}$. For $\varnothing\ne S\subseteq T$ the degree-$\boldsymbol\alpha$ part of $I_S/J_FI_S$ is
a full copy of $K$ exactly when $U\subseteq S$ (multiplication by $X_j$ reaches this degree from $I_S$ only when
$\alpha_j=1$ and $j\notin S$), and zero otherwise.

\emph{Case $U=\varnothing$.} $(V_F)_{\boldsymbol\alpha}=K$ and all faces survive; the augmented simplex is exact.
\emph{Case $U\ne\varnothing\ne T\setminus U$.} $(V_F)_{\boldsymbol\alpha}=0$, since subtracting $\mathbf e_j$ ($j\in U$)
leaves a nonzero vector; inserting some $t\in T\setminus U$ contracts the complex of faces $U\subseteq S\subseteq T$.
\emph{Case $T=U$, $|U|\ge2$.} Only $K$ in degree $|U|$ survives, with no incoming or outgoing maps
($(V_F)_{\mathbf e_U}=0$); this gives $K\langle\mathbf e_U\rangle$.
\emph{Case $T=U=\{j\}$.} In degree $\mathbf e_j$ the complex is $K\to(V_F)_{\mathbf e_j}=K/D$, because $J_F$ has
coefficient space $D$ (not $K$) at $\mathbf e_j$: the cofactor of $X_j$ is a constant. The map is the quotient map,
with kernel $D$ and zero cokernel; this gives $D\langle\mathbf e_j\rangle$ in degree one.
In degree $\mathbf 0$ only $D$ in degree zero remains.
\end{proof}

For $|S|=p\ge2$ the class of $k\in K$ is represented by $kX^{\mathbf e_S}$ in $I_S/J_FI_S$; in degree one only
coefficients $d\in D$ give cycles $dX_i$. Treating the constant coefficient as if it lay in $K$ gives a wrong
answer (12). Scaling coordinates by positive rationals $b_i$ ($i\in F$) is an automorphism of $A_\Lambda$ fixing the
coefficients, so the same formula holds for $A_\Lambda/(X_i^{b_i}:i\in F)$ with $\mathbf e_S$ replaced by
$\sum_{i\in S}b_i\mathbf e_i$ (12).

\begin{corollary}[Exact flat dimensions \src{12}]\label{osq:hd:cor:fd}
For $\Lambda=[d]$, $\fd_{A_d}D=d$ and $\fd_{A_d}\Pi_d=d-1$; for $\Lambda=\N_{>0}$ both are infinite. All positive
self-$\Tor$ groups $\Tor^{A_d}_{>0}(D,D)$ vanish.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The resolution gives $\fd D\le d$, and $\Tor_d(D,V_{[d]})$ is $D$ ($d=1$) or $K$ ($d\ge2$), nonzero. Truncation gives
$\fd\Pi_d\le d-1$; for $d\ge2$, $0\to\Pi_d\to A_d\to D\to0$ gives $\Tor_{d-1}(\Pi_d,V_{[d]})\cong\Tor_d(D,V_{[d]})\ne0$,
and for $d=1$, $\Pi_1=I_{\{1\}}$ is flat. In infinite rank take $|F|=n$ for every $n$. The last assertion is
\cref{osq:hd:cor:arithmeticTor}.
\end{proof}

For $d=2$ the groups are $D^2$ and $K$ (the latter represented by $kX_1X_2$), and for $d=3$ they are $D^3,K^3,K$:
vertices, edges and the full face (12). The finite audit of 12 checks this formula for $D=\Q\subset K=\Q(\sqrt2)$
(\cref{osq:sub:checks}).

\subsection{Coefficient lattices and their syzygies}\label{osq:tn:sub:lattices}

The next results need only a ring of the following shape, which covers 12's cores, 12's ordered-cone rings and 14's
rings.

\paragraph{Setting (L).} $K$ is a field, $D\subseteq K$ a unital subring, and $A=D\oplus\Pi$ is one of:
(a) $A_\Lambda$ with $\Pi=\Pi_\Lambda$; (b) $T_\Gamma$ with $\Pi=\Pi^{\mathrm{fin}}_\Gamma$; (c) the class ring
$\A_{D,K}$ with $\Pi=\Pi_K$ (full surreal exponents, $K$ a set field). In each case $B=K\oplus\Pi$ is a ring containing
$A$, $\Pi$ is an ideal of $B$ and a $K$-vector space, $K\cap\Pi=0$, all three lie in a field, and $\Pi$ is not finitely
generated in (a), (b) and not generated by a set in (c) (\cref{osq:hd:lem:faces}(iii), \cref{osq:hd:prop:etale},
\cref{osq:prop:common}(iii)). A \emph{coefficient lattice} is $M=Dm_1+\dots+Dm_r\subseteq K$ with $m_1,\dots,m_r$
linearly independent over $D$ (equivalently over $\Frac D$); $r=\rk M$. Put
\[
 L(M)=M+\Pi\subseteq B,\qquad J_g(M)=X^gL(M)\subseteq A
\]
for a nonzero monomial $X^g\in\Pi$ (a vector $g\ne\mathbf0$ in (a), $g\in\Gamma_{>0}$ in (b), $g>0$ in (c)). For
$(D,K)=(\Z,\R)$ or $(\Z[i],\C)$, $D$ is a principal ideal domain and every nonzero finitely generated $D$-submodule of
$K$ is free, so these are exactly 14's coefficient lattices.

\begin{theorem}[Generators and the first syzygy \src{14; 12; 17} \merge]\label{osq:tn:thm:syzygy}
In Setting (L), let $M$ have basis $m_1,\dots,m_r$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item $L(M)=\sum_jAm_j$, $\Pi L(M)=\Pi$ and $L(M)/\Pi L(M)\cong M$; the least number of generators of $L(M)$ is $r$,
although $L(M)$ has rank one over the fraction field.
\item The sequence
\begin{gather*}
 0\longrightarrow\Pi^{r-1}\xrightarrow{\ \sigma\ }A^r\xrightarrow{\ \pi\ }L(M)\longrightarrow0,\qquad
 \pi(a)=\sum_ja_jm_j,\\
 \sigma(u_2,\dots,u_r)=\Bigl(-\sum_{j\ge2}\tfrac{m_j}{m_1}u_j,\ u_2,\dots,u_r\Bigr),
\end{gather*}
is exact. For $r=1$, $L(M)\cong A$; for $r\ge2$, $L(M)$ is not finitely presented in (a), (b), and in (c) the kernel is
not generated by any set.
\item \emph{(12's certificate.)} Let $c\in K\setminus\Frac(D)$ and $H=(X^g,cX^g)A$. Then
$X^gA\cap cX^gA=X^g\Pi$ and $0\to\Pi\xrightarrow{m\mapsto(-cm,m)}A^2\xrightarrow{(a,b)\mapsto X^ga+cX^gb}H\to0$ is
exact; in (a) and (b), $A$ is not coherent.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
(i) Since $0\ne m_1\in K$ and $\Pi$ is a $K$-space, $m_1\Pi=\Pi$; so $\sum_jAm_j\supseteq M+m_1\Pi=L(M)$, and the
reverse inclusion is clear. $\Pi L(M)\subseteq\Pi$ because $\Pi$ is an ideal of $B$, and $\Pi L(M)\supseteq\Pi m_1=\Pi$.
As $M\cap\Pi\subseteq K\cap\Pi=0$, $L(M)/\Pi\cong M\cong D^r$. Generators of $L(M)$ give generators of the $D$-module
$L(M)/\Pi L(M)$, and a free module of rank $r$ over a nonzero commutative ring needs at least $r$ generators (reduce
modulo a maximal ideal).
(ii) If $\sum_ja_jm_j=0$ with $a_j=d_j+u_j$, the components in $K$ and $\Pi$ give $\sum_jd_jm_j=0$, so every $d_j=0$
by independence, and $u_1=-\sum_{j\ge2}(m_j/m_1)u_j$; conversely these tuples lie in $\ker\pi$, and $(m_j/m_1)u_j\in\Pi$.
$\sigma$ is injective. For $r\ge2$ the kernel projects onto $\Pi$, which is not finitely (set-) generated, and a
finitely presented module has finitely generated kernels for every surjection from a finite free module.
(iii) Apply (ii) to $M=D+Dc$ (the elements $1,c$ are independent over $D$ because $c\notin\Frac D$) and multiply by
$X^g$, which is injective: $H=X^gL(M)$. If $X^g(d+u)=cX^g(e+v)$ then $d=ce$ forces $d=e=0$, so
$X^gA\cap cX^gA=X^g(\Pi\cap c\Pi)=X^g\Pi$, not finitely generated; in a coherent ring the intersection of two finitely
generated ideals is finitely generated \cite[Tag~05CU]{StacksCoherent}.
\end{proof}

\Cref{osq:tn:thm:syzygy}(iii) is 12's noncoherence certificate for $A_\Lambda$ and $T_\Gamma$ with any unital $D\subseteq K$;
(i) and (ii) are 14's theorem for $\Oz$, $\GOz$ and $A_0$ with $D$ a principal ideal domain. Stating both at once in
Setting (L) is the merge's; the proofs are the sources'. All compatible relations between two generators with the same
monomial and incompatible constant lines are forced into the infinitely divisible tail (12). No novelty is attached to
noncoherence as such: rings of the form $D+M$ and their coherence have a long history, including Dobbs--Papick
\cite{DobbsPapick}; 14 uses no valuation-ring hypothesis on $B$, and the useful information is the exact syzygy (14).
Manuscript~17 proves (i), (ii) and the intersection formula of (iii) again, independently, for $\Oz$, $\GOz$ and the models
$R^{11}_{\Gamma_\kappa,<\kappa}$, and adds that in case (c) the module $L(M)$ with $r\ge2$ has no presentation by sets
(\cref{osq:rel:thm:presentation}); it credits the kernel shape $\Pi^{r-1}$ and the flat dimension one of the $D+M$ setting to
Dobbs \cite{Dobbs} and the intersection mechanism to Dobbs--Papick.

\begin{proposition}[Antisymmetric torsion and nonflatness \src{14} \merge]\label{osq:tn:prop:nonflat}
In Setting (L), let $M,N$ be coefficient lattices and $\mathsf m_{M,N}:M\otimes_DN\to K$ the multiplication map. On
$\mathsf E(M,N)=\Pi\oplus(M\otimes_DN)$ the rule
\begin{equation}\label{osq:tn:eq:action}
 (d+h)\cdot(u,t)=\bigl(du+hu+h\,\mathsf m_{M,N}(t),\ dt\bigr)\qquad(d\in D,\ h\in\Pi)
\end{equation}
is an $A$-module structure, and $(m+u)\otimes(n+v)\mapsto(mv+un+uv,\ m\otimes n)$ is a well-defined $A$-linear map
$\beta:L(M)\otimes_AL(N)\to\mathsf E(M,N)$. If $\rk M\ge2$ and $m_1,m_2$ are basis vectors, then
$\tau=m_1\otimes m_2-m_2\otimes m_1$ is a nonzero element of $L(M)\otimes_AL(M)$ with product $0$. Consequently, in
(a) and (b), $L(M)$ and every ideal $J_g(M)$ are not flat, hence neither projective nor invertible.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
For $a=d+h$, $b=e+g'$ in $A$ one computes that both $a\cdot(b\cdot(u,t))$ and $(ab)\cdot(u,t)$ have first coordinate
$abu+(dg'+he+hg')\mathsf m_{M,N}(t)$ and second coordinate $de\,t$; additivity and $1\cdot(u,t)=(u,t)$ are clear, and
$h\,\mathsf m_{M,N}(t)\in\Pi$ because $\Pi$ is a $K$-space. Write $x=m+u\in L(M)$, $y=n+v\in L(N)$ (uniquely, since
$M\cap\Pi=0$); the formula is $(xy-mn,\ m\otimes n)$, which is biadditive. For $a=d+h\in A$, the constant part of $ax$ is
$dm$, so the value at $(ax,y)$ is $(axy-dmn,\ dm\otimes n)$, which equals $a\cdot(xy-mn,m\otimes n)$ by
\eqref{osq:tn:eq:action}, and the value at $(x,ay)$ is the same. Hence $\beta$ exists. $\beta(\tau)=(0,\tau)$, and
$\tau\ne0$ in the free $D$-module $M\otimes_DM$ with basis $m_i\otimes m_j$. For flatness, $J=J_g(M)\cong L(M)$ is an
ideal of $A$ ($X^gM\subseteq\Pi$ and $X^g\Pi\subseteq\Pi$); tensoring the injection $J\hookrightarrow A$ with $J$ gives
the multiplication $J\otimes_AJ\to J$, which kills the nonzero element corresponding to $\tau$. Projective modules and
invertible ideals are flat.
\end{proof}

In (c) the tensor product is 14's explicit carrier (\cref{osq:tn:sub:tensor}); there the same element shows that
tensoring $J_g(M)\hookrightarrow A$ with $J_g(M)$ is not injective, which is 14's statement that $L(M)$ is not flat over
$\Oz$. Over $A_0$ this is 14's corollary; allowing any unital $D\subseteq K$ and 12's rings is the merge's (the proof is
14's, and no principal-ideal hypothesis is used in it).

\subsection{Flat and projective dimensions; the ordered cone}\label{osq:hd:sub:dimensions}

\begin{theorem}[Lattice modules and cyclic quotients \src{12, 14} \merge]\label{osq:hd:thm:fdlattice}
Let $A$ be $A_d$ ($d\ge1$) or $T_\Gamma$, and put $\delta=d$, respectively $\delta=1$. Let $M$ be a coefficient lattice of rank
$r\ge2$ and $J=J_g(M)$. Then
\[
 \fd_AL(M)=\delta,\qquad \fd_A(A/J)=\delta+1,
\]
and for every $A$-module $N$ and $j\ge2$,
$\Tor^A_j(L(M),N)\cong\Tor^A_j(D,N)^{r-1}\cong\Tor^A_{j+1}(A/J,N)$. Over $A_{\N_{>0}}$ both dimensions are infinite.
In particular, for $c\in K\setminus\Frac(D)$ and $H=(X_1,cX_1)$, $\fd_{A_d}(A_d/H)=d+1$ and $\fd_{T_\Gamma}(T_\Gamma/(X^g,cX^g))=2$,
and over $A_0=T_\Q$, $\fd L(M)=1$ and $\fd(A_0/J_e(M))=2$. If moreover $c^2\in D$, then
$\Tor_2^A(A/H,A/H)\cong D+cD\cong D\oplus D$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
By \cref{osq:tn:thm:syzygy}(ii) there is an exact sequence $0\to\Pi^{r-1}\to A^r\to L(M)\to0$, and $\fd\Pi=\delta-1$
(\cref{osq:hd:cor:fd}; for $T_\Gamma$, $\Pi$ is flat by \cref{osq:hd:thm:orderedcone} below). Hence $\fd L(M)\le\delta$ and, from
$0\to J\to A\to A/J\to0$ with $J\cong L(M)$, $\fd(A/J)\le\delta+1$. The long exact sequences give, for $j\ge2$,
$\Tor_j(L(M),N)\cong\Tor_{j-1}(\Pi,N)^{r-1}\cong\Tor_j(D,N)^{r-1}$ and $\Tor_{j+1}(A/J,N)\cong\Tor_j(J,N)$. If $\delta=d\ge2$,
$\Tor_d(D,V_{[d]})\cong K\ne0$ (\cref{osq:hd:thm:boolean}) gives both lower bounds. If $\delta=1$, $L(M)$ is not flat
(\cref{osq:tn:prop:nonflat}), so $\fd L(M)=1$ and $\Tor_2(A/J,N)\cong\Tor_1(J,N)\ne0$ for some $N$. In infinite rank,
$\Tor_n(D,V_F)\ne0$ for $|F|=n$. The special cases are $M=D+Dc$ with $J=H$, and $M$ of rank at least two over $T_\Q$. For
the last assertion (12), with $U=D+cD$ one has $H=UX^g\oplus X^g\Pi\subseteq\Pi$, $H\Pi=X^g\Pi$ (as $\Pi^2=\Pi$ and
$c\Pi=\Pi$) and $cH\subseteq H$ (as $c^2\in D$). The two sequences give
$\Tor_2(A/H,A/H)\cong\Tor_1(H,A/H)\cong\ker(\Pi/X^g\Pi\to(A/H)^2,\ m\mapsto(-cm,m))$, and the kernel consists of the
classes of $m\in H$, that is $H/X^g\Pi\cong U$.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{Correction to 12 and the relation to 14.} 12 states $\fd(A_d/H)=d+1$ and $\fd(T_\Gamma/H)=2$ under the extra
hypothesis $c^2\in D$, which it uses only to compute $\Tor_2(A/H,A/H)$; for $d\ge2$ its own shift argument never uses it,
and for $d=1$ and $T_\Gamma$ the lower bound $2$ follows from 14's antisymmetric torsion tensor. The hypothesis is needed only
for the formula $\Tor_2(A/H,A/H)\cong D\oplus D$. 14's flat-dimension proposition for $A_0$ is the case $A=T_\Q=A_1$ of the
theorem. Since a nonzero $\Tor$ in degree $n$ forces weak global dimension at least $n$: if $K\ne\Frac D$ (for instance
$(D,K)=(\Z,\R)$ or $(\Z[i],\C)$ with $c=\sqrt2$), then $\operatorname{w.gl.dim}A_d\ge d+1$,
$\operatorname{w.gl.dim}T_\Gamma\ge2$, and the countable-rank core has infinite weak global dimension (12, 14). Neither source
claims the exact weak global dimension of any of these rings.

\begin{proposition}[Projective dimensions \src{12} \merge]\label{osq:hd:prop:pd}
\leavevmode
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{(Telescope, 12.)} Let $A$ be a domain and $I=\bigcup_{n\ge0}x_nA$ for nonzero $x_n$ with $x_n=x_{n+1}u_n$,
$u_n\in A$. With $F=\bigoplus_{n\ge0}Ae_n$, the sequence
$0\to F\xrightarrow{\Delta}F\xrightarrow{\pi}I\to0$, $\Delta(e_na)=e_na-e_{n+1}u_na$, $\pi(e_na)=x_na$, is exact; so $\pd_AI\le1$.
This applies to every $I_S$ ($x_n=p_S^{2^{-n}}$) and to $\Pi^{\mathrm{fin}}_\Gamma$ ($x_n=X^{2^{-n}g}$, $g\in\Gamma_{>0}$).
\item \merge\ $\pd_{A_\Lambda}I_S=1$ and $\pd_{T_\Gamma}\Pi^{\mathrm{fin}}_\Gamma=1$.
\item $d\le\pd_{A_d}D\le d+1$ (12), and \merge\ $\pd_{A_1}D=\pd_{T_\Gamma}D=2$.
\item \merge\ Over $T_\Gamma$ (in particular over $A_0$), for a lattice of rank at least two, $\pd L(M)=2$ and
$\pd(T_\Gamma/J_g(M))=3$. Over $A_d$, $d\le\pd L(M)\le d+1$ and $d+1\le\pd(A_d/J_g(M))\le d+2$ (12, for $M=D+Dc$).
\item \merge\ Let $(A,I,D)$ satisfy (H1)--(H3) of \cref{osq:sec:homological}, with $A$ consisting of series of nonnegative
support (as do all rings of \cref{osq:sec:models} and $T_\Gamma$). If $I$ is countably generated, equivalently if $\Gamma_{>0}$ has a
countable coinitial subset, then $\pd_AI=1$ and $\pd_AD=2$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
(i) $\pi\Delta=0$ since $x_{n+1}u_n=x_n$; $\pi$ is onto. $\Delta$ is injective: at the least index $n_0$ of a nonzero vector the
coefficient is unchanged. If $\pi(v)=0$, use $e_na\equiv e_{n+1}u_na$ modulo $\operatorname{im}\Delta$ to move $v$ to a single index
$N$: $v\equiv e_Na$ with $x_Na=\pi(v)=0$, so $a=0$. For $T_\Gamma$, every tail has a positive exponent below its finitely many
exponents, hence below some $2^{-n}g$.
(ii) These ideals are nonzero and not finitely generated (\cref{osq:hd:lem:faces}(iii) and its analogue for $T_\Gamma$), so they
are not projective by \cref{osq:lem:projideal}.
(iii) Every $C_p$ of \cref{osq:hd:thm:resolution} has projective dimension at most $1$ by (i); splicing (or dimension
shifting along the resolution) gives $\pd D\le d+1$, and $\pd\ge\fd=d$. For $T_\Gamma$ (and $A_1\cong T_\Q$), $0\to\Pi\to T_\Gamma\to D\to0$
gives $\pd D\le\pd\Pi+1=2$; if $\pd D\le1$, $\Pi$ would be projective (Schanuel), against (ii).
(iv) $0\to\Pi^{r-1}\to T_\Gamma^r\to L(M)\to0$ gives $\pd L(M)\le2$; if $\pd L(M)\le1$, the kernel $\Pi^{r-1}$, hence its summand $\Pi$,
would be projective. So $\pd L(M)=2$, and since $J=J_g(M)\cong L(M)$ is not projective, $\pd(T_\Gamma/J)=\pd J+1=3$. Over $A_d$ the upper
bounds come from $\pd\Pi_d\le d$ (the truncated resolution, by (i)), and the lower bounds from
\cref{osq:hd:thm:fdlattice}, since $\pd\ge\fd$.
(v) If $I$ is generated by countably many $f_n=X^{g_n}b_n$ ((H2)), then for $h\in\Gamma_{>0}$ the element $X^h$ lies in
$\sum_{n\le N}f_nA\subseteq X^{g}A$ with $g=\min_{n\le N}g_n$, and $X^{h-g}\in A$ forces $g\le h$ because $A$ has no negative
exponents: the $g_n$ are coinitial.
Conversely, for a coinitial decreasing sequence $h_n$, (H2) gives $I=\bigcup_nX^{h_n}A$ with $X^{h_n}=X^{h_{n+1}}X^{h_n-h_{n+1}}$, a
countable chain as in (i). So $\pd I\le1$; $I$ is not projective by (H3) and \cref{osq:lem:projideal}; hence $\pd I=1$ and, as in
(iii), $\pd D=2$.
\end{proof}

Items (ii)--(v) sharpen 12's bounds $1\le\pd_{A_1}D\le2$ and are consistent with 06's $\pd\ge2$ (\cref{osq:thm:homological}(v)). For
$d\ge2$ the value $\pd_{A_d}D\in\{d,d+1\}$ is not determined. Among the five constructions of \cref{osq:sec:models}, (v) applies
exactly to $A^{08}_\kappa$ with $\cf\kappa=\aleph_0$: the tail of each construction needs as many generators as the length of its
coinitial chain (\cref{osq:rem:reconcile}), namely $\cf\kappa$ for 03 and 08, $\kappa$ for 06 and 11, $\aleph_1$ for 09, and only 08
allows countable cofinality. \Cref{osq:q:pd} is re-scoped accordingly.

\begin{theorem}[Ordered-cone cores \src{12}]\label{osq:hd:thm:orderedcone}
$(T_\Gamma,\Pi^{\mathrm{fin}}_\Gamma,D)$ satisfies (H1)--(H3) of \cref{osq:sec:homological}. Consequently
$\Pi^{\mathrm{fin}}_\Gamma=\bigcup_{\delta\in\Gamma_{>0}}X^\delta T_\Gamma$ is flat and idempotent but not projective,
$\Tor^{T_\Gamma}_{>0}(D,N)=0$ for every $D$-module $N$, $\fd_{T_\Gamma}D=1$ with $\Tor_1(D,T_\Gamma/X^\gamma T_\Gamma)\cong D$, and
the other items of \cref{osq:thm:homological} hold; $T_\Gamma\to D$ is formally \'etale and not flat, $\pd_{T_\Gamma}D=2$, and
$T_\Gamma$ is not coherent.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
(H1) is \cref{osq:hd:lem:cores}(i). For (H2), a nonzero tail $f$ has finitely many positive exponents; with $g$ half the least of
them, $f=X^g(X^{-g}f)$ and $X^{-g}f$ is again a tail, its coefficients being arbitrary in $K$. (H3) holds because a finite family of
tails has a least exponent among its monomials and cannot generate a monomial of smaller exponent. Apply
\cref{osq:thm:homological}, \cref{osq:hd:prop:etale}, \cref{osq:hd:prop:pd}(iii) and \cref{osq:tn:thm:syzygy}(iii).
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Same exponent group, different dimension \src{12}]\label{osq:hd:thm:collapse}
Let $(\gamma_i)_{i\in\Lambda}$ be positive reals linearly independent over $\Q$, $\Gamma=\sum_i\Q\gamma_i$, and
$A_\Lambda\hookrightarrow T_\Gamma$ the inclusion of \cref{osq:hd:lem:cores}(iii). Then $\Pi_\Lambda T_\Gamma=\Pi^{\mathrm{fin}}_\Gamma$
and $T_\Gamma\otimes_{A_\Lambda}D\cong D$. If $|\Lambda|\ge2$ the extension is not flat:
$(X_1A_\Lambda\cap X_2A_\Lambda)T_\Gamma\ne X_1T_\Gamma\cap X_2T_\Gamma$. The flat dimension of $D$ drops from $d$ to $1$ in rank $d$,
and from $\infty$ to $1$ in countable rank.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
For $\gamma\in\Gamma_{>0}$ choose a positive rational $q$ with $q\gamma_1<\gamma$; then
$kX^\gamma=X_1^q\cdot kX^{\gamma-q\gamma_1}\in\Pi_\Lambda T_\Gamma$, so $\Pi_\Lambda T_\Gamma=\Pi^{\mathrm{fin}}_\Gamma$ and
$T_\Gamma\otimes_{A_\Lambda}A_\Lambda/\Pi_\Lambda\cong T_\Gamma/\Pi^{\mathrm{fin}}_\Gamma=D$. Relabel so that $\gamma_1<\gamma_2$. Every monomial
of an element of $X_1A_\Lambda\cap X_2A_\Lambda$ has first and second coordinate at least $1$, hence real exponent at least
$\gamma_1+\gamma_2$, and multiplying by $T_\Gamma$ only adds nonnegative exponents; but
$X^{\gamma_2}=X_1X^{\gamma_2-\gamma_1}\in X_1T_\Gamma\cap X_2T_\Gamma$. A flat extension preserves finite intersections of ideals: tensor the
exact sequence $0\to I\cap I'\to A\to A/I\oplus A/I'$ with it. The dimensions are \cref{osq:hd:cor:fd} and
\cref{osq:hd:thm:orderedcone}.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}[Two directions inside $\Oz$ \src{12}]\label{osq:hd:ex:cone}
With $\gamma_1=1$, $\gamma_2=\sqrt2$ and $(D,K)=(\Z,\R)$, the core $A_2=\Z+\bigoplus_{a,b\in\Q_{\ge0},\,a+b>0}\R\,\omega^{a+b\sqrt2}$
lies in the ring $T_{\Q+\Q\sqrt2}\subseteq\Oz$, which also contains $\omega^{\sqrt2-1}\notin A_2$; the identity
$\omega^{\sqrt2}=\omega\cdot\omega^{\sqrt2-1}$ is the extra intersection element, and the two arithmetic quotients have flat
dimensions $2$ and $1$. Replacing $(\Z,\R)$ by $(\Z[i],\C)$ gives the same example in $\GOz$.
\end{example}

\begin{remark}[Flat dimension one is a property of the cone \merge]\label{osq:hd:rem:reconcile}
The report now contains two kinds of statements about the flat dimension of the arithmetic quotient, and they concern
different rings.
\begin{itemize}
\item $\fd_AD=1$ holds whenever (H1)--(H3) hold: the tail is a directed union of principal monomial ideals, because the
positive exponents form the positive cone of a totally ordered group and every family of tails of the relevant size
(finite for finite supports, of fewer than $\kappa$ or countably many elements in the constructions, a set for the class ring)
has a common monomial divisor in the ring. This covers the five constructions of \cref{osq:sec:models}
(\cref{osq:thm:homological}), including 06's ring $A^{06}_\kappa$; the full omnific ring in the two-universe reading, where
13 proves that the tail is flat, idempotent and not generated by a family from the smaller universe, that
$\Tor_{>0}(D,D)=0$ and that $D$ is not flat, so $\fd D=1$; 14's $A_0$; and 12's $T_\Gamma$
(\cref{osq:hd:thm:orderedcone}).
\item For the coordinatewise cone $\Q_{\ge0}^d$ the flat dimension is $d$ (\cref{osq:hd:cor:fd}): under the embedding, with
$\gamma_1<\gamma_2$, the quotient $\omega^{\epsilon(\gamma_2-\gamma_1)}$ of $X_2^\epsilon$ by $X_1^\epsilon$ lies in $\Oz$ but not in
$A_d$, so the tail of $A_d$ is not a directed union of principal ideals; it is a sum of $d$ flat ideals that is not flat for
$d\ge2$.
\end{itemize}
So ``flat dimension one'' must never be asserted for an arbitrary subring of $\Oz$: $A_d\subseteq T_\Gamma\subseteq\Oz$, with all exponents
in one Archimedean class, have flat dimensions $d$ and $1$, and $A_d\to T_\Gamma$ is not flat. Being a subring of a ring with small
quotient flat dimension imposes no bound on the subring, and conversely the cores say nothing about the homological dimensions of
the full class ring $\Oz$, which none of the sources computes (12, 14). The projective dimension of $D$ behaves differently
again: under (H1)--(H3) it is $2$ when the tail is countably generated, in particular for $T_\Gamma$, and is otherwise known only
to be at least~$2$; for $A_d$ with $d\ge2$ it is $d$ or $d+1$ (\cref{osq:hd:prop:pd}).
\end{remark}

\subsection{The tensor normal form}\label{osq:tn:sub:tensor}

For the rest of this section (14) fix $(D,k)=(\Z,\R)$ or $(\Z[i],\C)$, and let $(A,\Pi,B)$ be either the class rings
$(\A_{D,k},\Pi_k,\A_{k,k})$, that is $A=\Oz$ or $\GOz$, or the set-sized model $(T_\Q,\Pi^{\mathrm{fin}}_\Q,k+\Pi^{\mathrm{fin}}_\Q)$
with $T_\Q=A_0$. Coefficient lattices are those of Setting (L). Every result below holds in both cases (14); its proofs use
only that $D$ is a principal ideal domain in $k$, that $A=D\oplus\Pi$ with $\Pi$ a $k$-linear ideal of $B$, and that every
$u\in\Pi$ is a product $u=ca$ with $c,a\in\Pi$ (\cref{osq:prop:common}(ii); in $T_\Q$ split off a smaller monomial). This
interface is 14's proposal for a formalization (\cref{osq:sub:formalization}). For the class rings a tensor product means an
explicit carrier with the balanced universal property proved by finite identities (for two lattices,
$\Pi\times(M\otimes_DN)$); no quotient of a proper class by a class relation and no class colimit is used (14).

\begin{lemma}[Absorption \src{14}]\label{osq:tn:lem:absorption}
For every lattice $N$ and $0\ne n_0\in N$, multiplication is an isomorphism $\Pi\otimes_AL(N)\cong\Pi$ with inverse
$u\mapsto(u/n_0)\otimes n_0$; indeed $u\otimes v=(uv/n_0)\otimes n_0$ for $u\in\Pi$, $v\in L(N)$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Write $u=ca$ with $c,a\in\Pi$. Since $v/n_0\in B$, $b=a(v/n_0)\in\Pi\subseteq A$, and
$u\otimes v=c\otimes av=c\otimes bn_0=cb\otimes n_0=(uv/n_0)\otimes n_0$. So every finite tensor sum is the image of its
product, multiplication is onto ($u=(u/n_0)n_0$), and the two maps are mutually inverse. For a balanced bilinear map $b'$ into
a class module, $u\mapsto b'(u/n_0,n_0)$ is the required factorization, unique by the same identity.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Exact tensor normal form \src{14; 17}]\label{osq:tn:thm:tensor}
The map $\beta$ of \cref{osq:tn:prop:nonflat} is an $A$-module isomorphism $L(M)\otimes_AL(N)\cong\mathsf E(M,N)$. Multiplication
corresponds to $(u,t)\mapsto u+\mathsf m_{M,N}(t)$, and there is an exact sequence
\[
 0\longrightarrow\mathcal T(M,N)\longrightarrow L(M)\otimes_AL(N)\longrightarrow L(MN)\longrightarrow0,\qquad
 \mathcal T(M,N)=\ker\bigl(M\otimes_DN\to MN\bigr),
\]
with $\mathcal T(M,N)$ embedded as $(0,\mathcal T(M,N))$. This is exactly the $A$-torsion submodule; if it is nonzero its annihilator is
exactly $\Pi$, and $\rk_D\mathcal T(M,N)=\rk M\cdot\rk N-\rk(MN)$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Fix $0\ne n_0\in N$, let $j(w)=(w/n_0)\otimes n_0$ on $\Pi$ and let $s$ be induced by the constant inclusions on $M\otimes_DN$. Then
$(u,t)\mapsto j(u)+s(t)$ is inverse to $\beta$: in an expanded pure tensor the terms with an infinite first factor reduce by
\cref{osq:tn:lem:absorption}, a term $m\otimes v$ with $v=ca\in\Pi$ equals $cm\otimes a$, and all mixed terms become
$j(mv+un+uv)$, while the constant term is $s(m\otimes n)$. The same finite identities give the universal property for balanced maps
into class modules, so this carrier is the tensor product in the class case. The image of multiplication is $MN+\Pi=L(MN)$, and since
$\Pi\cap k=0$ its kernel is $(0,\mathcal T(M,N))$, killed by $\Pi$ by \eqref{osq:tn:eq:action}; the quotient lies in a field and is
torsion free, so the kernel is all the torsion. It is a submodule of a finite free $D$-module, hence free, and a nonzero $d\in D$
kills none of its nonzero elements. Ranks: $M\otimes_DN\to MN$ is onto.
\end{proof}

For $M=D+D\alpha$ with $\alpha\notin\Frac D$, $\tau=1\otimes\alpha-\alpha\otimes1$ is a nonzero tensor with $h\tau=0$ for all
$h\in\Pi$; if $\alpha^2=c\in D$, then $\tau$ and $\alpha\otimes\alpha-c(1\otimes1)$ form a $D$-basis of the multiplication kernel (14).
Manuscript~17 proves the exact sequence and the torsion statement independently over its set-sized models, as a consequence of
its $\Tor$ identification (\cref{osq:rel:thm:tor}).
Here ``torsion'' means $A$-torsion: $\mathcal T(M,N)$ is a free abelian group (14).

\begin{proposition}[Several factors \src{14}]\label{osq:tn:prop:higher}
For lattices $M_1,\dots,M_n$, $\bigotimes_{j,A}L(M_j)\cong\Pi\oplus\bigotimes_{j,D}M_j$ with the action \eqref{osq:tn:eq:action} for
the $n$-fold product map; the torsion is its kernel, killed by $\Pi$. For equal factors, permutations act trivially on the first
coordinate and by permuting tensor factors on the second.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Expand a pure tensor: the all-constant term keeps its $D$-tensor coordinate, and every other term contains a factor from $\Pi$ and
reduces, by repeated absorption (moving a tail scalar to an adjacent factor), to its product in one copy of $\Pi$. So the multilinear
map has coordinates $\bigl(\prod_j(m_j+u_j)-\prod_jm_j,\ \bigotimes_jm_j\bigr)$, with inverse as in \cref{osq:tn:thm:tensor}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Exterior and symmetric powers \src{14}]\label{osq:tn:thm:powers}
Let $r=\rk M$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item For $j\ge2$, $\bigwedge^j_AL(M)\cong\bigwedge^j_DM$ with $A$ acting through $\ct$: a free $D$-module of rank $\binom rj$ with
annihilator $\Pi$ when nonzero.
\item For $n\ge1$, $\operatorname{Sym}^n_AL(M)\cong\Pi\oplus\operatorname{Sym}^n_DM$ with the action \eqref{osq:tn:eq:action} for the
product map $\mathsf m_n:\operatorname{Sym}^n_DM\to k$; the product map has image $L(M^n)$ ($M^n$ the $D$-span of $n$-fold products) and
torsion kernel $\ker\mathsf m_n$, of rank $\binom{r+n-1}n-\rk(M^n)$, annihilated by $\Pi$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
(i) For $u\in\Pi$ and $0\ne m\in M$, $u/m\in A$ and $u\wedge m=(u/m)m\wedge m=0$; for $u,v\in\Pi$ both are multiples of one
$m_0$, so $u\wedge v=0$. Hence every wedge with a tail factor vanishes, $h(m_1\wedge\dots\wedge m_j)=0$ for $h\in\Pi$, the constant
inclusion induces a surjection $\bigwedge^j_DM\to\bigwedge^j_AL(M)$, and taking constants in all factors is an alternating
$A$-multilinear left inverse. (ii) By \cref{osq:tn:prop:higher} a permutation difference has the form $(0,t-\sigma t)$, with product
zero, so multiplying it by an element of $\Pi$ gives zero and imposes no relation on the first coordinate; the quotient is
$\Pi\oplus\operatorname{Sym}^n_DM$, and the kernel argument of \cref{osq:tn:thm:tensor} applies.
\end{proof}

A rank-one ideal of $\Oz$ can thus have a nonzero $r$th exterior power for every $r\ge2$; these powers vanish after passing to the
fraction field, in agreement with the field-valued rank (14).

\subsection{Rees equations}\label{osq:tn:sub:rees}

For $e>0$ the Rees algebra of $L(M)$ is $\mathcal R(L(M))=A\oplus\bigoplus_{n\ge1}L(M)^nT^n\subseteq F[T]$ ($F$ the fraction field);
degreewise multiplication by $X^{ne}$ identifies it, and the symmetric algebra, with those of the ideal $J_e(M)$
\cite[Tag~052P]{StacksRees}. An ideal is \emph{of linear type} if $\operatorname{Sym}\to\mathcal R$ is an isomorphism, and the
\emph{first nonlinear degree} is the least degree of a nonzero element of the kernel after the linear relations are imposed (14).

\begin{theorem}[All nonlinear Rees equations are arithmetic \src{14}]\label{osq:tn:thm:rees}
Let $m_1,\dots,m_r$ be a basis of $M$ and $\mathfrak q_M=\ker\bigl(D[X_1,\dots,X_r]\to k[T],\ X_i\mapsto m_iT\bigr)$. The
constant-coefficient map $D[X_1,\dots,X_r]\to\operatorname{Sym}_AL(M)$ is injective, and under it
$\ker(\operatorname{Sym}_AL(M)\to\mathcal R(L(M)))=\mathfrak q_M$; this is all the $A$-torsion of the symmetric algebra, it is killed
by $\Pi$, and it is generated by any set of homogeneous generators of $\mathfrak q_M$, finitely many of which suffice. With
$\mathfrak l_M=\bigl(hX_j-\tfrac{m_j}{m_1}hX_1:h\in\Pi,\ 2\le j\le r\bigr)\subseteq A[X_1,\dots,X_r]$,
\[
 \operatorname{Sym}_AL(M)\cong A[\mathbf X]/\mathfrak l_M,\qquad
 \mathcal R(L(M))\cong A[\mathbf X]/(\mathfrak l_M+\mathfrak q_MA[\mathbf X]).
\]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The symmetric algebra is presented by the linear relations of the generator map \cite[Tag~00DM]{StacksTensor}, which are those of
\cref{osq:tn:thm:syzygy}(ii). In degree $n\ge1$, \cref{osq:tn:thm:powers}(ii) identifies the constant polynomials with
$\operatorname{Sym}^n_DM$ and the kernel with $\ker\mathsf m_n$, the degree-$n$ part of $\mathfrak q_M$; in degree $0$ the map is the
identity of $A$. The quotient embeds in the domain $F[T]$, so the kernel is all the torsion. A polynomial in $A[\mathbf X]$ acts on the
kernel through its coefficientwise constant term, so generators over $D[\mathbf X]$ generate the kernel, and $D[\mathbf X]$ is
Noetherian.
\end{proof}

The nonlinear kernel is finitely generated while, for $r\ge2$, the linear relation module is not finitely (set-) generated
(\cref{osq:tn:thm:syzygy}); the two statements are compatible (14).

\begin{theorem}[The algebraic degree is the first nonlinear degree \src{14}]\label{osq:tn:thm:degree}
Let $\alpha\in k\setminus\Frac(D)$, $M=D+D\alpha$ and $J=(X^e,\alpha X^e)\subseteq A$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item If $\alpha$ is transcendental over $\Frac(D)$, then $J$ is of linear type, although it is not flat and not finitely presented.
\item If $\alpha$ is algebraic of degree $d$ (necessarily $d\ge2$), let $f\in D[Z]$ be primitive irreducible with $f(\alpha)=0$ and
$F_f(X,Y)=X^df(Y/X)$. Then $\mathcal R(J)\cong A[X,Y]/\bigl(hY-\alpha hX\ (h\in\Pi),\ F_f(X,Y)\bigr)$; the kernel of
$\operatorname{Sym}_AJ\to\mathcal R(J)$ is generated by $F_f$, and the first nonlinear degree is exactly $d$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
A homogeneous $G$ of degree $n$ lies in $\mathfrak q_M$ iff $G(1,\alpha)=0$. In the transcendental case $G=0$. In the algebraic case the
minimal polynomial of $\alpha$ divides $g(Z)=G(1,Z)$ over $\Frac D$, and Gauss's lemma for the unique factorization domain $D$ and the
primitivity of $f$ give $g=fh$ with $h\in D[Z]$, $\deg h\le n-d$; homogenizing, $G=F_f\cdot X^{n-d}h(Y/X)$. Conversely $F_f(1,\alpha)=0$.
Apply \cref{osq:tn:thm:rees}; nonflatness and failure of finite presentation are \cref{osq:tn:prop:nonflat,osq:tn:thm:syzygy}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[A family of every degree \src{14; 17}]\label{osq:tn:cor:alldegrees}
For $d\ge2$ let $J_d=(\omega,2^{1/d}\omega)\subseteq\Oz$. Then
