\begin{equation}\label{osq:pm:eq:normalizeddifference}
 \frac{Q_F(u,v)}{a_dT^{d-1}}
 =\sum_{\ell=0}^{d-1}z^{d-1-\ell}w^\ell
 +\sum_{j=1}^{d-1}b_j\sum_{\ell=0}^{j-1}z^{j-1-\ell}w^\ell .
\end{equation}
Its standard part is $d\zeta^{d-1}$, of modulus $d$ because $\zeta^d=1$; by
\cref{osq:pm:lem:finiteroot} the standard part of its modulus is $d$, so
\[
 |Q_F(u,v)|>\frac d2|a_d|T^{d-1}.
\]
If $u\ne v$, the triangle inequality gives $|F(u)-F(v)|\le2H$, hence
\[
 |u-v|=\frac{|F(u)-F(v)|}{|Q_F(u,v)|}<\frac{4H}{d|a_d|T^{d-1}}<1,
\]
the middle quantity being infinitesimal by \eqref{osq:pm:eq:scaling}. This contradicts
lattice separation for $0\ne u-v\in\Lambda$ (\cref{osq:pm:lem:lattice}). So $u=v$, and
\eqref{osq:pm:eq:preimages} has at most $q_{E,d}$ elements. Every image point in the ball
is the image of an element of \eqref{osq:pm:eq:preimages}, which gives the last assertion.
\end{proof}

The proof uses the finite residue polynomial $Z^d-1$ only as a label for possible clusters.
It assumes no convergence of an iterative root-finding scheme, no compactness of an
omnific interval, and no existence theorem for new roots in the full field.

\begin{theorem}[Arbitrary-scale holes in nonlinear polynomial images \src{10}]
\label{osq:pm:thm:holes}
Let $F\in\Num_1(\Lambda)$ have degree $d\ge2$. For every $H>0$ and every $B\ge0$ in $\No$
there is $c\in\Lambda$ with $|c|>B$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{osq:pm:eq:hole}
 \{y\in E:|y-c|\le H\}\cap F(\Lambda)=\varnothing .
\end{equation}
For the real lattice this is an empty closed interval; for the Gaussian lattice it is an
empty closed surcomplex disk.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Put $q=q_{E,d}$ and choose, by \cref{osq:pm:lem:lattice}, a positive $M\in\Oz$ with $M>H$.
Let $L=M+1$ and $R=3qL+M$. Apply \cref{osq:pm:thm:localcount} with radius $R$ to get
$T_0$, and choose $T\in\Oz$ with $T>T_0$ and also
\[
 |F(T)|>B+3qL .
\]
This is possible: by \eqref{osq:pm:eq:polyest}, for $T\ge\max(1,R_F)$ one has
$|F(T)|\ge\frac12|a_d|T^d\ge\frac12|a_d|T$, so any $T\in\Oz$ exceeding $T_0$, $1$, $R_F$
and $2(B+3qL)/|a_d|$ works (\cref{osq:pm:lem:lattice}).

Set $y=F(T)\in\Lambda$ and consider the $q+1$ lattice points $c_j=y+3jL$,
$j=0,\dots,q$. The closed balls of radius $M$ about them are pairwise disjoint, since
distinct centres are at distance at least $3L>2M$. Each lies in the closed ball of radius
$R$ about $y$, since $|x-y|\le|x-c_j|+3jL\le M+3qL$. That ball contains at most $q$ points
of $F(\Lambda)$ by \cref{osq:pm:thm:localcount}, so one of the $q+1$ smaller balls
contains none. Its centre $c=c_j$ satisfies $|c|\ge|y|-3qL>B$, and since $H<M$ the ball
of radius $H$ about $c$ is disjoint from $F(\Lambda)$ as well.
\end{proof}

Failure of surjectivity is therefore not a single small defect near zero: the gaps exceed
any prescribed infinite surreal radius and occur beyond any prescribed bound in modulus,
that is, at arbitrarily large moduli.

\begin{corollary}[All rational bijections of the two lattices \src{10; 16; 18}]
\label{osq:pm:cor:bijections}
A rational function $F\in E(X)$ defining a bijection $\Lambda\to\Lambda$ has the form
\[
 F(X)=uX+b,\qquad b\in\Lambda,\quad u\in\Lambda^\times,
\]
and every such map is a bijection. Thus the rational bijections of $\Oz$ are
$X\mapsto\pm X+b$ with $b\in\Oz$, and those of $\GOz$ are $X\mapsto uX+b$ with
$b\in\GOz$ and $u\in\{\pm1,\pm i\}$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
By \cref{osq:pm:thm:rational}, $F$ is a polynomial, so $F\in\Num_1(\Lambda)$. A constant is
not a bijection of $\Lambda$. Degree at least two is excluded by \cref{osq:pm:thm:holes},
because the centre $c$ there is itself a lattice point missing from the image. Hence
$F(X)=aX+b$ with $b=F(0)\in\Lambda$ and $a=F(1)-F(0)\in\Lambda$. Surjectivity gives
$x\in\Lambda$ with $F(x)=b+1$, so $ax=1$ and $a$ is a unit of $\Lambda$. Conversely, an
affine map with slope $u\in\Lambda^\times$ has the lattice-preserving inverse
$X\mapsto u^{-1}(X-b)$. The unit lists are those of \cref{osq:lem:units}.
\end{proof}

Here ``rational'' means a rational function in one variable over the ambient field $E$.
Arbitrary functions $\Lambda\to\Lambda$, and maps such as coordinatewise conjugation on
$\GOz$, are not being classified. 16 shows that surjectivity alone already forces the affine form, for
every unital constant ring (\cref{osq:fs:thm:surjective}).

\subsection{Fresh scales: image gaps, fibers and surjective self-maps}\label{osq:fs:sub:gaps}

This subsection and the next are manuscript~16's. They work over an arbitrary constant ring and replace the
lattice-separation mechanism of \cref{osq:pm:sub:rigidity} by a \emph{fresh scale}: an exponent above an entire
set-sized group of old exponents. Throughout, $k\in\{\R,\C\}$, $E_k=\hahn k\No$ (so $E_\R=\No$, $E_\C=\SC$), $D\subseteq k$ is a unital
subring, and $\A_{D,k}=D+\Pi_k$ as in \cref{osq:sub:rings}; $\A_{\Z,\R}=\Oz$ and $\A_{\Z[i],\C}=\GOz$. The ring $\A_{k,k}=k+\Pi_k$ of normal
forms without negative exponents (16's $\mathcal I_k$) is \emph{not} the ring $\OO_k$ of finite surreals: $\omega\in\A_{k,k}$ and
$\omega^{-1}\notin\A_{k,k}$. 16's $\mathcal P_k$ and $\mathcal A_D$ are $\Pi_k$ and $\A_{D,k}$; its defect polynomial $D_{\zeta,c}$ is
$\mathsf d_{\zeta,c}$ (the letter $D$ is the constant ring), its centre $\mu$ is $s$, its affine part $L_\zeta$ is $\mathsf a_\zeta$ and its
forbidden coefficient $A_\zeta$ is $\mathsf c_\zeta$. For a set-sized additive subgroup $G\subseteq\No$, $K_G=\hahn kG$ is the Hahn field
with supports in $G$, identified with its normal-form image in $E_k$ ($X^g\mapsto\omega^g$).

\begin{lemma}[Fresh exponents \src{16}]\label{osq:fs:lem:fresh}
Every set-sized family of elements of $E_k$ lies in $K_G$ for some set-sized subgroup $G\subseteq\No$. There is $b>0$ with
$b>g$ for all $g\in G$ (a \emph{fresh} exponent), and then $jb+g>0$ for $j>0$ and $jb+g<0$ for $j<0$ ($j\in\Z$, $g\in G$); in
particular $G\cap\Z b=\{0\}$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Let $G$ be the subgroup generated by the union of the supports; it is a set, and $K_G$ is a set-sized Hahn field. Choose
$b$ strictly above the set $G\cup\{0\}$ (\cref{osq:lem:gap}); since $G=-G$, $b+g>0>-b+g$, and the same follows for all
nonzero integral multiples of $b$.
\end{proof}

Freshness dominates the whole group, not only finitely many leading exponents: to dominate $G=\Q$ one may take $b=\omega$,
while no ordinary real suffices (16).

\begin{theorem}[Laurent blocks \src{16}]\label{osq:fs:thm:embedding}
Let $b>G$ be fresh and $t=\omega^b$. The map
$\iota_b:K_G(\!(T^{-1})\!)\to E_k$, $\sum_{j\le m}c_jT^j\mapsto\sum_{j\le m}c_j\omega^{jb}$, is an injective field
homomorphism, the identity on $K_G$, with $\iota_b(T)=t$. Each nonzero $c_j$ occupies the support block $jb+\supp c_j$; blocks
with different $j$ are disjoint and ordered by $j$. In particular $t$ is transcendental over $K_G$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
For $j<\ell$ and $g,h\in G$, $(\ell b+h)-(jb+g)=(\ell-j)b+(h-g)>0$. A nonempty subset of the union of the blocks has a greatest
element: take the greatest outer index of a block meeting it (the indices are bounded above), then the greatest element in that
block. So the image is a legitimate Hahn sum even when the $c_j$ have infinite supports. Addition is coefficientwise; in a product
a fixed outer index receives contributions from finitely many pairs of outer indices, each an ordinary Hahn product. A nonzero
coefficient gives a nonempty block that cannot cancel, so $\iota_b$ is injective; a unital injective ring map from a field preserves
inverses, and injectivity on $K_G[T]$ is transcendence.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Exact support test \src{16}]\label{osq:fs:cor:blocktest}
For $L=\sum_{j\le m}c_jT^j$, $\iota_b(L)\in\A_{D,k}$ if and only if $c_j=0$ for all $j<0$ and $c_0\in\A_{D,k}$; the same holds with
$\A_{k,k}$ in place of $\A_{D,k}$. Positive-index blocks are purely infinite.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Negative-index blocks consist of negative exponents and cannot cancel; the block of index $0$ is $c_0$; positive-index blocks do not
affect the negative terms or the constant term.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[One fresh value forces polynomiality \src{16}]\label{osq:fs:thm:onepoint}
Let $F\in K_G(T)$. Its denominator does not vanish at $t$, and $F(t)\in\A_{D,k}$ if and only if $F\in K_G[T]$ and the constant
coefficient of $F$ lies in $\A_{D,k}$. The same holds with $t$ replaced by $ut$ for any $u\in K_G^\times$; these inputs lie in $\Pi_k$.
If the proper part of $F$ begins $aT^{-n}+O(T^{-n-1})$ with $a\ne0$, $n\ge1$, then $F(t)$ has the nonzero block $-nb+\supp a$, and if
the constant coefficient of the polynomial part has no negative terms, the leading negative exponent of $F(t)$ is $\max\supp a-nb$,
with the leading coefficient of $a$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Transcendence gives the first assertion. Expand $F$ at infinity by polynomial division and a geometric inverse; its image under $\iota_b$ is
$F(t)$, and a rational function has no negative Laurent coefficients exactly when it is a polynomial. Apply
\cref{osq:fs:cor:blocktest}. Substituting $uT$ multiplies $c_j$ by $u^j$ and fixes $c_0$; $ut$ occupies the positive block $b+G$. The
last assertion is the block order of \cref{osq:fs:thm:embedding}.
\end{proof}

One fresh value detects negative Laurent tails, not integer-valuedness: $F(X)=X/2$ passes the test but fails at $X=1$ (16).

\begin{lemma}[Branches of a shifted value \src{16}]\label{osq:fs:lem:branches}
Let $d\ge2$, let $K$ be a field of characteristic $0$ containing the $d$th roots of unity, and
$f=a_dX^d+\dots+a_0\in K[X]$ with $a_d\ne0$. For every $c\in K$ the equation $f(Y)=f(T)+c$ has exactly $d$ distinct roots in
$K(\!(T^{-1})\!)$, indexed by $\zeta^d=1$, of the form
\[
 Y_{\zeta,c}=\zeta T+(\zeta-1)s+O(T^{-1}),\qquad s=\frac{a_{d-1}}{da_d}.
\]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
With $u=T^{-1}$ and $W=uY$, multiplying by $u^d$ gives $\sum_ja_ju^{d-j}(W^j-1)-cu^d=0$, which at $u=0$ is $a_d(W^d-1)=0$ with simple
roots $\zeta$ (the $W$-derivative there is $da_d\zeta^{d-1}\ne0$). The coefficient of $u^n$ is then solved uniquely step by step, giving
$W_\zeta\in K[[u]]$ with $W_\zeta(0)=\zeta$, and $Y=TW_\zeta(T^{-1})$; the $d$ distinct leading coefficients give $d$ distinct roots, which exhaust
the roots of a polynomial of degree $d$. Comparing coefficients of $T^{d-1}$ in $f(\zeta T+w+\dots)=f(T)+c$ gives
$da_d\zeta^{d-1}w+a_{d-1}\zeta^{d-1}=a_{d-1}$, that is $w=(\zeta-1)s$.
\end{proof}

Put $\mathsf a_\zeta(T)=\zeta T+(\zeta-1)s$ and $\mathsf d_{\zeta,c}(T)=f(\mathsf a_\zeta(T))-f(T)-c$. The choices of $\zeta$ and $s$ cancel
the terms of degrees $d$ and $d-1$, so $\deg\mathsf d_{\zeta,c}\le d-2$ unless $\mathsf d_{\zeta,c}=0$. Since $\mathsf a_\zeta$ fixes $-s$,
\begin{equation}\label{osq:fs:eq:defect}
 \mathsf d_{\zeta,c}(-s)=-c:
\end{equation}
a nonzero output shift cannot be produced by an affine change of input, even if the coefficients of $f$ are infinite or
infinitesimal (16). Degrees and leading coefficients $\lc$ below refer to the formal variable $T$.

\begin{theorem}[Forbidden layers \src{16}]\label{osq:fs:thm:forbidden}
In \cref{osq:fs:lem:branches} let $c\ne0$. For each $\zeta^d=1$ put $q_\zeta=\deg\mathsf d_{\zeta,c}$, $r_\zeta=d-1-q_\zeta$ and
$\mathsf c_\zeta=-\lc(\mathsf d_{\zeta,c})/(da_d\zeta^{d-1})$. Then $\mathsf d_{\zeta,c}\ne0$, $1\le r_\zeta\le d-1$, $\mathsf c_\zeta\ne0$ and
\[
 Y_{\zeta,c}=\mathsf a_\zeta(T)+\mathsf c_\zeta T^{-r_\zeta}+O(T^{-r_\zeta-1});\qquad\text{in particular}\qquad
 Y_{1,c}=T+\frac{c}{da_d}T^{1-d}+O(T^{-d}),
\]
so the bound $d-1$ is attained for every $c\ne0$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
By \eqref{osq:fs:eq:defect} the defect is nonzero, of degree between $0$ and $d-2$. The remainder $R=Y_{\zeta,c}-\mathsf a_\zeta$ lies in
$T^{-1}K[[T^{-1}]]$ and is nonzero; let $\chi T^m$ ($m\le-1$) be its first term. Taylor expansion gives
$0=\mathsf d_{\zeta,c}+f'(\mathsf a_\zeta)R+\sum_{j\ge2}f^{(j)}(\mathsf a_\zeta)R^j/j!$. The linear term has degree $d-1+m$ and leading coefficient
$da_d\zeta^{d-1}\chi$, while the $j$th term has degree at most $d-j+jm<d-1+m$ because $(j-1)(1-m)>0$. Hence $q_\zeta=d-1+m$ and
$\lc(\mathsf d_{\zeta,c})+da_d\zeta^{d-1}\chi=0$. For $\zeta=1$, $\mathsf a_1=T$ and $\mathsf d_{1,c}=-c$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Uniform image gap \src{16}]\label{osq:fs:thm:imagegap}
Let $f\in K_G[X]$ have degree $d\ge2$, let $b>G$ be fresh and $t=\omega^b$. Then
\[
 f(\A_{k,k})\cap\bigl(f(t)+K_G\bigr)=\{f(t)\},\qquad\text{hence}\qquad f(\A_{D,k})\cap\bigl(f(t)+K_G\bigr)=\{f(t)\}.
\]
For every $0\ne c\in K_G$, every root in $\SC$ of $f(Y)=f(t)+c$ has a nonzero block $-r_\zeta b+\supp\mathsf c_\zeta$ of negative
exponents, with $1\le r_\zeta\le d-1$ as in \cref{osq:fs:thm:forbidden}.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Work over $\hahn\C G$ if $k=\R$ (the roots of unity are ordinary constants, so no new exponents arise). \Cref{osq:fs:lem:branches} and
\cref{osq:fs:thm:embedding} give $d$ distinct roots of $f(Y)-f(t)-c$ in $\SC$, hence all of them. For $c\ne0$ each has, by
\cref{osq:fs:thm:forbidden}, a nonzero coefficient in a block of negative index, which lies below exponent $0$ and cannot cancel against the
affine constant or another block; so no root lies in $\A_{\C,\C}$, and none in $\A_{k,k}\supseteq\A_{D,k}$. For $c=0$, $t\in\Pi_k$ gives the value
$f(t)$.
\end{proof}

The same $b$ works for every $c\in K_G$ at once, and $G$ need not be Archimedean, countable or divisible (16).

\begin{remark}[Special cases printed elsewhere \src{16; 08}]\label{osq:fs:rem:special}
Three nonsolvability statements proved separately are instances of \cref{osq:fs:thm:imagegap} with $G=\{0\}$, where every $b>0$ is
fresh and $K_G=k$. (a) $x^2=\omega^2+1$ has no solution in $\Oz$: take $f=X^2$, $t=\omega$, $c=1$; this is an example of the
Diophantine report \cite{RepoOdg}. (b) For $a\in\R_{>0}$, $\gamma>0$, $0\ne\beta\in\Z$ and $n\ge2$, $X^n=a\omega^\gamma+\beta$ has
no solution in $\Oz$: put $X=a^{1/n}Y$ with $Y\in\A_{\R,\R}$, $f=aY^n$, $t=\omega^{\gamma/n}$, $c=\beta$; this is the two-term root obstruction of
the Diophantine report \cite{RepoOdg}, whose short proof by the binomial expansion is kept there. (c) For $\gamma>0$ and $m\ge2$,
$X^m=\omega^\gamma+1$ has no root in $\GOz$: take $k=\C$, $f=X^m$, $t=\omega^{\gamma/m}$, $c=1$; this is 08's invisible equation
(\cref{osq:nm:thm:invisible}), whose explicit expansion \eqref{osq:nm:eq:rootexpansion} exhibits the forbidden block of layer $r_1=m-1$.
\Cref{osq:fs:thm:imagegap} is also consistent with 10's local count (\cref{osq:pm:thm:localcount}): for fresh $t$ the fiber of $f(t)$ has
at most $e(f)\le d$ points in the complex case and at most one ($d$ odd) or two ($d$ even) in the real case (\cref{osq:fs:thm:fiber} below),
within 10's bound $q_{E,d}$.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}[Omnific non-solvability \src{16}]\label{osq:fs:cor:diophantine}
If $f$ maps $\A_{D,k}$ into itself and has degree at least two, then with $K_G$ and $t$ as above, $f(Y)=f(t)+c$ has no solution
$Y\in\A_{D,k}$ for any $0\ne c\in K_G\cap\A_{D,k}$, although its right side lies in $\A_{D,k}$. For $f\in k[X]$ one may take $G=\{0\}$ and any
$b>0$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{definition}[Centered polynomial \src{16}]\label{osq:fs:def:centered}
For $f$ of degree $d\ge2$ with centre $s=a_{d-1}/(da_d)$ write $h(U)=f(U-s)=\sum_{j=0}^dh_jU^j$ (so $h_{d-1}=0$) and
$e(f)=\gcd\{j\ge1:h_j\ne0\}$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Exact fresh fibers \src{16}]\label{osq:fs:thm:fiber}
For $\zeta^d=1$, $f(\mathsf a_\zeta(T))=f(T)$ if and only if $h(\zeta U)=h(U)$ if and only if $\zeta^{e(f)}=1$; if this fails, the branch
$Y_{\zeta,0}$ has a nonzero negative Laurent term, and if it holds, $Y_{\zeta,0}=\mathsf a_\zeta$. Consequently, for $f\in K_G[X]$ of degree at
least two and fresh $t$,
\[
 \{y\in\A_{D,k}:f(y)=f(t)\}=\bigl\{\zeta t+(\zeta-1)s:\ \zeta\in k,\ \zeta^{e(f)}=1,\ (\zeta-1)s\in\A_{D,k}\bigr\},
\]
distinct $\zeta$ giving distinct points. If $s$ has a nonzero negative-exponent part the fiber is $\{t\}$; if $s\in\Pi_k$, all $e(f)$ points occur
when $k=\C$, and one or two when $k=\R$, according as $e(f)$ is odd or even.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
$\mathsf a_\zeta(T)+s=\zeta(T+s)$ gives the first equivalence; the second is $\zeta^j=1$ for all supported $j$, that is $\zeta^{e(f)}=1$ by
B\'ezout. If the defect vanishes, $\mathsf a_\zeta$ is a root with leading coefficient $\zeta$, hence the branch by uniqueness of the simple-root
lift; otherwise the remainder is nonzero and the argument of \cref{osq:fs:thm:forbidden} applies. Every root is a branch; nonsymmetric branches
have a forbidden block; a symmetric one is $\zeta t+(\zeta-1)s$, whose fresh term cannot affect the old negative terms or the constant, so it lies in
$\A_{D,k}$ exactly when $(\zeta-1)s$ does (and $\zeta\in k$: a nonreal $\zeta$ gives a nonreal leading coefficient). Distinct $\zeta$ have distinct
fresh leading coefficients; multiplying a nonzero negative part of $s$ by $\zeta-1\ne0$ keeps it; the count is that of roots of unity in $k$.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}[Worked fibers and defects \src{16}]\label{osq:fs:ex:worked}
(a) For $f=X^d$, $s=0$ and $e(f)=d$: the fresh fiber of $t^d$ in $\GOz$ is $\{\zeta t:\zeta^d=1\}$ (the constant term of $\zeta t$ is $0$, so
$\zeta$ need not be a Gaussian integer), and for $0\ne c\in k$ the shifted roots are
$\zeta t(1+ct^{-d})^{1/d}=\zeta t+\frac{\zeta c}{d}t^{1-d}+\frac{\zeta(1-d)c^2}{2d^2}t^{1-2d}+\cdots$, with first negative layer exactly
$d-1$ (a formal binomial expansion followed by the block embedding). (b) $f=X^3+X^2$ has $s=\tfrac13$, $h=U^3-\tfrac13U+\tfrac2{27}$ and
$e(f)=1$, so $f(t)$ has the single preimage $t$ in $\Oz$ and in $\GOz$; this does not make $f$ injective ($f(0)=f(-1)$). (c) $f=\omega X^2+X$
has the infinitesimal centre $1/(2\omega)$; at $t=\omega^\omega$ the other algebraic preimage of $f(t)$ is $-t-\omega^{-1}$, and $f$ is in fact
injective on $\Oz$ and $\GOz$, since $f(x)=f(y)$, $x\ne y$, forces $x+y=-\omega^{-1}$. (d) For $f=X^3+\lambda X+\nu$ ($s=0$),
$\mathsf d_{\zeta,c}=\lambda(\zeta-1)T-c$: the identity branch first detects the shift at layer two ($\frac c3T^{-2}$), a nontrivial branch
with $\lambda\ne0$ at layer one ($-\lambda(\zeta-1)T^{-1}/(3\zeta^2)$). (e) For $f=X^4+pX^2+qX+r$,
$\mathsf d_{\zeta,c}=p(\zeta^2-1)T^2+q(\zeta-1)T-c$; the first negative terms are $cT^{-3}/4$ at $\zeta=1$, $-qT^{-2}/2$ at $\zeta=-1$ ($q\ne0$) and
$ipT^{-1}/2$ at $\zeta=i$ ($p\ne0$); when a displayed coefficient vanishes the next term of the finite defect decides. (f) Freshness cannot be
weakened casually: with $G=\Q$, $F(T)=\omega/T$ takes the value $1$ at the non-fresh $T=\omega$; and $f=X^2$, $t=\omega$ with the shift
$2\omega+1$, which is not below a fresh exponent, gives $(t+1)^2$, an omnific square.
\end{example}

\begin{theorem}[Rational self-maps over any constant ring \src{16}; second route to \cref{osq:pm:thm:rational}]\label{osq:fs:thm:collapse}
Let $F\in E_k(X)$ be a rational function, in lowest terms, whose values lie in $\A_{D,k}$ at every point of $\A_{D,k}$ where it is defined.
Then $F$ is a polynomial. Hence the rational self-maps of $\A_{D,k}$ are exactly its integer-valued polynomials.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Put the finitely many coefficients in some $K_G$ (\cref{osq:fs:lem:fresh}) and take a fresh $t=\omega^b\in\Pi_k\subseteq\A_{D,k}$. The
denominator does not vanish at $t$, and $F(t)\in\A_{D,k}$, so $F$ is a polynomial by \cref{osq:fs:thm:onepoint}.
\end{proof}

For $D=\Z,\Z[i]$ this is 10's \cref{osq:pm:thm:rational}; the two proofs are different routes. 10's uses finite differences and
lattice separation ($|x|\ge1$ for $0\ne x\in\Oz$), which fails for $D=\Q$ or $D=k$; 16's uses one fresh input and works for every $D$. The
bare statement that integer-valued rational functions are polynomials is not new in principle: it is the D-ring phenomenon of
Gunji--McQuillan \cite{GunjiMcQuillan}, as recalled by Liu \cite[Proposition~1.12]{Liu}; for $\Oz$ and $\GOz$, whose unit groups are finite,
a nonconstant polynomial cannot take all its values in the unit group, which is the link with that criterion. 16 avoids importing a
theorem about set-sized domains into a proper-class ring and supplies an explicit fresh witness (16).

\begin{theorem}[Surjective rational self-maps \src{16}; second route to \cref{osq:pm:cor:bijections}]\label{osq:fs:thm:surjective}
A rational function over $E_k$ that is defined at every point of $\A_{D,k}$ and maps $\A_{D,k}$ into itself is surjective onto $\A_{D,k}$
if and only if it is $X\mapsto uX+a$ with $a\in\A_{D,k}$ and $u\in D^\times$; every such map is
bijective, and under composition they form the affine group $\A_{D,k}\rtimes D^\times$, $(a,u)(a',u')=(a+ua',uu')$. For $\Oz$ these are
$X\mapsto\pm X+a$, and for $\GOz$ they are $X\mapsto uX+a$ with $u\in\{\pm1,\pm i\}$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
By \cref{osq:fs:thm:collapse} the map is a polynomial; constants are not surjective. If its degree is at least two, then for a fresh $t$ the
element $F(t)+1\in\A_{D,k}$ is not a value, by \cref{osq:fs:thm:imagegap}. So $F=uX+a$ with $a=F(0)$ and $u=F(1)-F(0)$ in $\A_{D,k}$, and
surjectivity at $a+1$ gives $ux=1$ with $x\in\A_{D,k}$, so $u\in D^\times$ (\cref{osq:lem:units}). The inverse is $X\mapsto u^{-1}(X-a)$.
\end{proof}

For $\Oz$ and $\GOz$ this contains 10's classification of the rational bijections; 16 assumes only surjectivity and allows every unital
$D\subseteq k$, and its proof uses the image gap where 10's uses holes of every radius (\cref{osq:pm:thm:holes}). Both are kept.

\begin{corollary}[Omitted values form a proper class \src{16}]\label{osq:fs:cor:omitted}
If $F$ is an integer-valued polynomial of $\A_{D,k}$ of degree at least two, then $\A_{D,k}\setminus F(\A_{D,k})$ is a proper class; changing
$F$ on a set of inputs cannot make it surjective.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Given a set $S\subseteq\A_{D,k}$, enlarge $G$ to contain the supports of $S$ and of the coefficients of $F$ and take $b>G$. The omitted value
$F(\omega^b)+1$ has leading exponent $db+g_d$ ($g_d$ the leading exponent of the leading coefficient), which is not in $G$, so it is not in $S$.
Changing $F$ on a set adds at most a set of values.
\end{proof}

Complex conjugation bijects $\GOz$ but is not a rational function of one surcomplex variable (a rational function agreeing with $X$ on the
ordinary integers is $X$); admitting $F(\overline X)$ adds exactly the maps $u\overline X+a$. The theorem is one-variable:
$(x,y)\mapsto(x+y^2,y)$ is a nonlinear polynomial bijection of $\A_{D,k}^2$ (16).

\subsection{Finite tests, fresh probes and sampling}\label{osq:fs:sub:tests}

For a unital $D\subseteq k$ and $r\ge1$ put $\Int(D^r)=\{g\in\Frac(D)[\mathbf X]:g(D^r)\subseteq D\}$ and
$\Num_r(\A_{D,k})=\{F\in E_k[\mathbf X]:F(\A_{D,k}^r)\subseteq\A_{D,k}\}$, so that $\Num_r(\Oz)$ and $\Num_r(\GOz)$ are those of
\cref{osq:sec:polynomial}.

\begin{proposition}[Constant-ring transfer \src{16}; 10 for {$D=\Z,\Z[i]$}]\label{osq:fs:prop:transfer}
For every unital $D\subseteq k$ and $r\ge1$, $\Num_r(\A_{D,k})=\Int(D^r)\oplus\Pi_k[\mathbf X]$ additively, the second summand an ideal,
and the coefficientwise constant term is a ring homomorphism onto $\Int(D^r)$, split by inclusion.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Let $F\in\Num_r(\A_{D,k})$ have degree at most $d$ in each variable. The Newton coefficients \eqref{osq:pm:eq:newtoncoef} are integral
combinations of values at $\{0,\dots,d\}^r$, hence lie in $\A_{D,k}$, and $(d!)^r\binom{\mathbf X}{\mathbf k}\in\Z[\mathbf X]$, so every monomial
coefficient of $F$ lies in $\Frac(D)+\Pi_k$. Split $F=g+p$ with $g\in\Frac(D)[\mathbf X]$, $p\in\Pi_k[\mathbf X]$. The constant term is a ring
homomorphism on $\Frac(D)+\Pi_k$ (\cref{osq:lem:ct}), so for $\mathbf a\in D^r$, $g(\mathbf a)=\ct(F(\mathbf a))\in D$; thus $g\in\Int(D^r)$.
Conversely, for $\mathbf x=\mathbf a+\mathbf z$ with $\mathbf a\in D^r$, $\mathbf z\in\Pi_k^r$, every term of $g(\mathbf a+\mathbf z)-g(\mathbf a)$
contains a factor $z_j$, so it lies in the ideal $\Pi_k$ of $\A_{k,k}$, and $p(\mathbf x)\in\Pi_k$; hence $F(\mathbf x)\in g(\mathbf a)+\Pi_k$.
Uniqueness is $k\cap\Pi_k=0$, and the section is the inclusion.
\end{proof}

For $D=\Z$ and $\Z[i]$ this is \cref{osq:pm:thm:numreal,osq:pm:thm:numgaussian} (10, and independently 18 in $n$ variables); 16 proves it for $r=1$ and for several variables with
any $D$. The finite-difference identity needs no convergence argument: both sides agree at the grid points (16).

\begin{theorem}[Optimal point tests \src{16}; 10, 18]\label{osq:fs:thm:lower}
Let $L\subseteq\N^n$ be a finite nonempty lower set ($\boldsymbol\alpha\in L$ and $\boldsymbol\beta\le\boldsymbol\alpha$ coordinatewise imply
$\boldsymbol\beta\in L$) and $V_L=\Span_{\No}\{\mathbf X^{\boldsymbol\alpha}:\boldsymbol\alpha\in L\}$. For $F\in V_L$ the following are
equivalent: $F(\Oz^n)\subseteq\Oz$; $F(\boldsymbol\alpha)\in\Oz$ for all $\boldsymbol\alpha\in L$; all coefficients of the expansion
$F=\sum_{\boldsymbol\alpha\in L}c_{\boldsymbol\alpha}\binom{\mathbf X}{\boldsymbol\alpha}$ lie in $\Oz$. Moreover $|L|$ is the least size of a set of
points of $\Oz^n$, arbitrary omnific points allowed, whose values test membership for all of $V_L$. In particular, for one variable and degree at
most $d$, the $d+1$ points $0,\dots,d$ suffice and no $d$ points do.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The $\binom{\mathbf X}{\boldsymbol\alpha}$, $\boldsymbol\alpha\in L$, form a basis of $V_L$: each has leading monomial
$\mathbf X^{\boldsymbol\alpha}/\prod_j\alpha_j!$ and its other monomials have smaller indices, which stay in $L$. In a linear extension of the
coordinatewise order the evaluation matrix $\bigl(\binom{\boldsymbol\beta}{\boldsymbol\alpha}\bigr)$ on $L$ is integral and unitriangular, so
$c_{\boldsymbol\alpha}=\sum_{\boldsymbol\beta\le\boldsymbol\alpha}(-1)^{|\boldsymbol\alpha|-|\boldsymbol\beta|}\prod_j\binom{\alpha_j}{\beta_j}F(\boldsymbol\beta)$.
Each $\binom{X_j}{\alpha_j}$ preserves $\Oz$ (\cref{osq:pm:lem:binomial}). If fewer than $|L|$ points are proposed, their evaluation maps have a
common nonzero kernel element $h\in V_L$; a polynomial vanishing on $\Z^n$ is zero, so $h(\mathbf n)\ne0$ for some $\mathbf n\in\Z^n$, and
$h/(2h(\mathbf n))$ passes all proposed tests but takes the value $\tfrac12$ at $\mathbf n$.
\end{proof}

For rectangular $L$ the equivalences are 10's \cref{osq:pm:thm:grid}; general lower sets (for instance total degree at most $d$, with
$\binom{n+d}n$ points) and the optimality statement are 16's; 18 proves the total-degree case and the binomial basis independently. So the binomial polynomials form a basis of the $\Oz$-module $\Num_1(\Oz)$, each
element having finitely many nonzero coordinates, and the countable set $\Z$ tests all of $\Num_1(\Oz)$; no topological density is asserted
(16).

\begin{proposition}[A finite Gaussian test \src{16}]\label{osq:fs:prop:gausstest}
Let $F\in\SC[\mathbf X]$ have total degree at most $d$, $M=d!$ and $C_M=\{a+bi:a,b\in\Z,\ 0\le a,b<M\}$. Then $F$ preserves $\GOz$ if and
only if its values lie in $\GOz$ on $L_d\cup C_M^{\,n}$, where $L_d=\{\boldsymbol\alpha\in\N^n:|\boldsymbol\alpha|\le d\}$. This is an explicit
finite certificate, not a claim of minimal size.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The values on $L_d$ give $F=g+p$ with $g\in M^{-1}\Z[i][\mathbf X]$ and $p\in\Pi_\C[\mathbf X]$ (the denominators $\prod_j\alpha_j!$ divide $d!$).
With $h=Mg\in\Z[i][\mathbf X]$, $g(\mathbf z)\in\Z[i]$ means $h(\mathbf z)\equiv0\pmod{M\Z[i]}$, which depends only on the residues of the
coordinates, and $C_M$ is a complete residue system. So $g\in\Int(\Z[i]^n)$; apply \cref{osq:fs:prop:transfer}.
\end{proof}

The ordinary grid alone is not enough over $\GOz$: $\binom i2=(-1-i)/2$ (\cref{osq:pm:rem:gaussnewton}).

\begin{lemma}[Independent fresh coordinates \src{16}]\label{osq:fs:lem:independent}
For a set-sized subgroup $G$ there are positive $b_1,\dots,b_n$ whose classes are linearly independent over $\Q$ modulo $G$, and the
$t_j=\omega^{b_j}$ are algebraically independent over $K_G$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Choose $b_j$ above the set-sized rational span of $G,b_1,\dots,b_{j-1}$; a nontrivial relation would contradict the freshness of the last
participating $b_j$. Distinct monomials $\prod_jt_j^{\alpha_j}$ have exponents in distinct cosets of $G$, so their $K_G$-multiples have disjoint
supports.
\end{proof}

With such $b_j$, let $H=G+\sum_j\Q b_j$, choose $B>H$, and let $\xi^{(i)}\in\A_{D,k}^n$ have $i$th coordinate $\omega^B$ and $j$th coordinate
$\omega^{b_j}$ for $j\ne i$.

\begin{theorem}[Coordinate-dominant probes \src{16}]\label{osq:fs:thm:probes}
If $F\in K_G(X_1,\dots,X_n)$ and $F(\xi^{(i)})\in\A_{D,k}$ for $1\le i\le n$, then $F\in K_G[X_1,\dots,X_n]$. Consequently every rational
function mapping $\A_{D,k}^n$ into $\A_{D,k}$ at its points of definition is a polynomial.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Fix $i$ and view $F$ as a rational function of $X_i$ over $E_i=K_G(X_j:j\ne i)$. Substituting $X_j=t_j$ ($j\ne i$) embeds $E_i$ in $K_H$
(\cref{osq:fs:lem:independent}); a reduced numerator and denominator stay coprime (their B\'ezout identity survives) and keep their degrees. As
$\omega^B$ is fresh over $K_H$, \cref{osq:fs:thm:onepoint} makes the denominator constant in $X_i$, so $F\in E_i[X_i]$ for each $i$. If $F=P/Q$ in
lowest terms in the unique factorization domain $K_G[\mathbf X]$ had nonconstant $Q$, an irreducible factor of positive degree in some $X_i$
would remain a denominator over $E_i$ (Gauss's lemma).
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Workspace-uniform rational certificates \src{16}]\label{osq:fs:cor:certificates}
Fix $K_G$ and $d\ge0$. For rational functions over $K_G$ with numerator of total degree at most $d$ in lowest terms, being a self-map of
$\Oz^n\to\Oz$ is tested by $L_d$ together with the $n$ probes $\xi^{(i)}$ (at most $\binom{n+d}n+n$ values; $d+2$ for $n=1$), and of
$\GOz^n\to\GOz$ by $L_d\cup C_{d!}^{\,n}$ together with the probes.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The probes force a polynomial, of total degree at most $d$; then apply \cref{osq:fs:thm:lower} or \cref{osq:fs:prop:gausstest}. Necessity is
clear since all test points lie in the domain.
\end{proof}

These certificates are uniform for a whole coefficient workspace $K_G$; they are relative to exact coefficient data, zero tests and a certificate
of freshness, and are not an algorithm for arbitrary presentations of surreal coefficients (16).

\begin{theorem}[No set-sized sample tests all rational maps \src{16}]\label{osq:fs:thm:nosmall}
Let $\Lambda$ be $\Oz$ or $\GOz$ and $S\subseteq\Lambda$ a set. There is $\beta>0$ such that $R_S(X)=\omega^\beta/(X-\frac12)$ is defined on all of
$\Lambda$, satisfies $R_S(S)\subseteq\Pi_k$, and $R_S(\omega^{2\beta})=\sum_{m\ge0}2^{-m}\omega^{-(2m+1)\beta}\notin\Lambda$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
$\tfrac12\notin\Lambda$. Clear the set of supports of the $(s-\tfrac12)^{-1}$, $s\in S$ (\cref{osq:prop:fractions}(i)) to get $\beta$. At
$x=\omega^{2\beta}$, $R_S(x)=\omega^{-\beta}(1-\tfrac12\omega^{-2\beta})^{-1}$ is a nonzero infinitesimal.
\end{proof}

Thus no set of points, with all values known, detects all rational self-maps, even among functions of numerator degree $0$ and denominator
degree $1$ with no pole on $\Lambda$: the single function $\omega/(X-\frac12)$ takes purely infinite values at every ordinary and Gaussian
integer, yet $\omega/(\omega^2-\frac12)=\omega^{-1}+\frac12\omega^{-3}+\cdots\notin\Oz$. For polynomials, by contrast, the ordinary integers
suffice in every degree (\cref{osq:fs:thm:lower}) (16).

\begin{theorem}[Co-small integrality \src{16}]\label{osq:fs:thm:cosmall}
If $F\in E_k(X)$ is defined and $\A_{D,k}$-valued outside a set-sized subset of $\A_{D,k}$, then $F$ is an integer-valued polynomial of
$\A_{D,k}$: a rational function that is not a self-map fails on a proper class of inputs (poles counted as failures).
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Let $S$ contain all failures and put its supports and the coefficients of $F$ into $K_G$. If $F$ is not a polynomial, a fresh $t\notin S$
gives $F(t)\notin\A_{D,k}$ (\cref{osq:fs:thm:onepoint}). If $F$ is a polynomial with $F(a)\notin\A_{D,k}$, enlarge $G$ by the supports of $a$;
the constant coefficient of $F(a+T)$ is $F(a)$, so $F(a+t)\notin\A_{D,k}$ by \cref{osq:fs:cor:blocktest}, and $a+t\notin S$.
\end{proof}

\Cref{osq:fs:thm:nosmall,osq:fs:thm:cosmall} do not conflict: the first builds a bad function adapted to a set of successful tests, the second
says its failures form a proper class; neither provides one set meeting the failures of every function (16).

\begin{proposition}[Hulls \src{16}]\label{osq:fs:prop:hulls}
For $S\subseteq\A_{D,k}$ let $\operatorname{Hull}_{\mathrm{rat}}(S)$ be the set of $a\in\A_{D,k}$ such that every rational function over $E_k$
defined and $\A_{D,k}$-valued on $S$ is defined and $\A_{D,k}$-valued at $a$ (poles off $S$ allowed), and define
$\operatorname{Hull}_{\mathrm{pol}}$ with polynomials. Then $\operatorname{Hull}_{\mathrm{rat}}(S)=S$ for every set $S$, while
$\operatorname{Hull}_{\mathrm{pol}}(\Z)=\Oz$ and $\operatorname{Hull}_{\mathrm{pol}}(\Z[i])=\GOz$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
For $a\notin S$, clearing the supports of the $(s-a)^{-1}$ gives $\beta$ with $\omega^\beta/(X-a)$ purely infinite on $S$ and a pole at $a$.
The polynomial statements follow from \cref{osq:fs:prop:transfer}: values on $D$ force the ordinary part into $\Int(D)$, and the purely infinite
part is harmless.
\end{proof}

The pole allowed in the hull is not what drives \cref{osq:fs:thm:nosmall}, whose functions have no pole on the ring (16). In a fixed smaller Hahn
field whose value group has no exponent above the coefficient group, the fresh-scale argument cannot be reused; this is one of 16's questions
(\cref{osq:fs:q:fresh}).

\subsection{Ordinary outputs, canonical translations and composition}\label{osq:or:sub:outputs}

This subsection is manuscript~18's new material. Its description of $\Num_r(\Oz)$ and $\Num_r(\GOz)$, its simplex test for $\Oz^n$ (the
lower set $L_d$ of \cref{osq:fs:thm:lower}) with the binomial basis, its rational collapse and its classification of surjective rational
self-maps are 10's and 16's results proved again independently; they are credited at
\cref{osq:pm:thm:numreal,osq:pm:thm:numgaussian,osq:fs:thm:lower,osq:pm:thm:rational,osq:pm:cor:bijections}. Throughout,
$(E,\Lambda,D)$ is $(\No,\Oz,\Z)$ or $(\SC,\GOz,\Z[i])$, $\Pi_\Lambda$ is $\Pi$ or $\Pi_\C$, and $F_0=\Frac D$. 18's ideal $\mathcal J$, ring $A$,
field $K$, field $k_0$ and class $\Int(A)$ are $\Pi_\Lambda$, $\Lambda$, $E$, $F_0$ and $\Num_1(\Lambda)$; its reduction $\overline f$ is $\ct_*(f)$; its
$\operatorname{pi}(a)=a-\ct(a)$ is written $\mathring a$ (for $a\in F_0+\Pi_\Lambda$); and its \emph{ordinary-output locus}
$E_f=f^{-1}(D)$ and target loci $E_b(f)=f^{-1}(b+D)$ are $\mathcal L_f$ and $\mathcal L_{f,b}$ (the letter $E$ is the field). The coefficients of every
$f\in\Num_1(\Lambda)$ lie in $F_0\oplus\Pi_\Lambda$ (\cref{osq:pm:thm:numreal,osq:pm:thm:numgaussian}), so $\ct_*(f)\in\Int(D)$ and $\mathring a$ are
defined for them.

The first results hold for an abstract \emph{discretely ordered ring} $B$, an ordered domain in which $1$ is the least positive element. Put
$(R_*,D_*)=(B,\Z)$ or $(B[i],\Z[i])$; an element of an ordered field extension of $\Frac B$, or of its quadratic extension with the modulus
$|x+iy|=\sqrt{x^2+y^2}$ computed in a real closure, is \emph{finite} if its modulus is bounded by an ordinary integer and \emph{infinitesimal} if it
is below every $1/n$.

\begin{lemma}[Discrete lattices \src{18}]\label{osq:or:lem:discrete}
The finite elements of $B$ are the ordinary integers, and distinct elements of $B$ are at distance at least $1$; the same holds for $B[i]$ with
$\Z[i]$ and the modulus. $B_\Q=\{b/n:b\in B,\ n\ge1\}$ and $B[i]_\Q$ contain no nonzero infinitesimal. For $B=\Oz$ this is
\cref{osq:pm:lem:lattice}.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
A finite $b$ lies between two consecutive ordinary integers or equals one, and discreteness excludes the first case; apply this to differences.
Both coordinates of a finite Gaussian element are finite, and a nonzero difference has a coordinate of modulus at least $1$. A nonzero $b/n$ has
modulus at least $1/n$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Coefficient gap and one cluster \src{18}]\label{osq:or:lem:cluster}
\leavevmode
\begin{enumerate}
\item If $\deg f\le d$ and $f(0),\dots,f(d)\in R_*$, then all coefficients of $f$ lie in $(R_*)_\Q$; a nonzero leading coefficient is not infinitesimal.
\item If $f$ has degree $d\ge1$ and a leading coefficient that is not infinitesimal, and $x_0,\dots,x_d\in R_*$ are distinct with all $f(x_j)$ finite,
then all differences $x_i-x_j$ are finite.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
(i) Lagrange interpolation at $0,\dots,d$ has rational basis coefficients; apply \cref{osq:or:lem:discrete}. (ii) If the nodes fell into at least two
classes of finite distance, then for each $i$ some $|x_i-x_j|$ would be infinite and the others at least $1$, so every summand of
\[
 \lc(f)=\sum_{i=0}^d\frac{f(x_i)}{\prod_{j\ne i}(x_i-x_j)},
\]
the coefficient of $X^d$ in Lagrange interpolation, would be infinitesimal, and so would $\lc(f)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Sharp ordinary-output dichotomy \src{18}]\label{osq:or:thm:dichotomy}
Let $f$ be a polynomial of degree $d\ge1$ with $f(R_*)\subseteq R_*$, and $\mathcal L_f=\{x\in R_*:f(x)\in D_*\}$. Exactly one of the following holds:
\begin{enumerate}
\item $\#\mathcal L_f\le d$;
\item $f(X)=g(X-a)$ for some $a\in R_*$ and $g\in\Int(D_*)$ of degree $d$, and then $\mathcal L_f=a+D_*$, a coset determined by $f$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Let $\mathcal L_f$ contain $d+1$ points. By \cref{osq:or:lem:cluster}(i) the leading coefficient is not infinitesimal, and by (ii) the points lie in
one class of finite distance: they are $a+n_j$ with $n_j\in D_*$ (\cref{osq:or:lem:discrete}). Then $g(X)=f(X+a)$ takes values in $D_*$ at the $d+1$
points $n_j$, so $g\in\Frac(D_*)[X]$; for every $n\in D_*$, $g(n)=f(a+n)\in R_*$ is an ordinary rational (or Gaussian rational) element of $R_*$, hence
finite, hence in $D_*$. So $g\in\Int(D_*)$ and $a+D_*\subseteq\mathcal L_f$. If $x\in\mathcal L_f$ and $y=x-a$ were infinite, the ordinary
coefficients of $g$ would give $|g(y)|\ge\frac12|\lc(g)||y|^d$, which is infinite, although $g(y)=f(x)\in D_*$; so $y$ is finite and lies in $D_*$.
The alternatives exclude each other because $D_*$ is infinite.
\end{proof}

The clustering and the descent to $\Frac(D_*)[X]$ use only $f(0),\dots,f(d)\in R_*$ and $d+1$ ordinary outputs; the step $g\in\Int(D_*)$ uses
preservation of all of $R_*$. For $\Oz$ that global hypothesis is itself finitely testable (\cref{osq:fs:thm:lower}); for a general discretely ordered
ring this must not be assumed (18).

\begin{corollary}[\src{18}]\label{osq:or:cor:trace}
$f(R_*)\cap D_*$ has at most $d$ elements or equals $g(D_*)$ with $g$ as in \cref{osq:or:thm:dichotomy}. If $R_*\ne D_*$, no nonconstant polynomial
preserving $R_*$ maps $R_*$ into $D_*$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Apply $f$ to $\mathcal L_f$. If $\mathcal L_f=R_*$, then $R_*$ is infinite and equals a coset $a+D_*$ containing $0$, so $R_*=D_*$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[The bound is sharp \src{18}]\label{osq:or:prop:sharp}
If $B$ has a positive infinite element $H$, then for $d\ge2$ the polynomial $\prod_{j=1}^d(X-jH)$, and for $d=1$ the polynomial $HX$, preserves $B$ and
$B[i]$, and its ordinary-output locus consists of exactly $d$ points ($H,\dots,dH$, respectively $0$).
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
For $d\ge2$ the $d$ roots lie in different classes of finite distance, so by \cref{osq:or:thm:dichotomy} there is no further point. If $Hx$ is finite and
$x\ne0$, then $|x|\ge1$ and $|Hx|$ is infinite.
\end{proof}

The degree-one replacement is necessary: $X-H$ has the locus $H+D_*$. The bound concerns inputs, not distinct outputs: all $d$ roots above go to $0$
(18).

\begin{theorem}[Canonical translation \src{18}]\label{osq:or:thm:canonical}
Let $f\in\Num_1(\Lambda)$ have degree $d\ge1$. Either $\#f^{-1}(D)\le d$, or there is a unique $\eta\in\Pi_\Lambda$ with
\[
 f(X)=\ct_*(f)(X-\eta),
\]
and then $\deg\ct_*(f)=d$ and $f^{-1}(D)=\eta+D$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
$\Oz$ is discretely ordered (\cref{osq:pm:lem:lattice}); apply \cref{osq:or:thm:dichotomy}. In its second case write $a=c+\eta$ with $c\in D$ and
$\eta\in\Pi_\Lambda$, so $f(X)=h(X-\eta)$ with $h(Y)=g(Y-c)\in\Int(D)$. The coefficients of $h(X-\eta)$ are polynomials in $\eta$ with coefficients
in $F_0$, and $\ct$ is a ring homomorphism on $F_0+\Pi_\Lambda$ with $\ct(\eta)=0$, so $\ct_*(f)=h$. If $\eta+D=\eta'+D$ then $\eta-\eta'\in D\cap\Pi_\Lambda=0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The two highest coefficients \src{18}]\label{osq:or:thm:eta}
Let $f=\sum_{j\le d}a_jX^j\in\Num_1(\Lambda)$ with $a_d\ne0$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item If $a_d\notin F_0$, then $\#f^{-1}(D)\le d$.
\item If $a_d\in F_0$, put $\eta_f=-\mathring a_{d-1}/(da_d)\in\Pi_\Lambda$. Then $f^{-1}(D)$ is infinite if and only if $f(X+\eta_f)=\ct_*(f)(X)$; otherwise
it has at most $d$ elements.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
In the canonical form the leading coefficient is that of $\ct_*(f)$, which is in $F_0$; this proves (i). Comparing coefficients of $X^{d-1}$ in
$f(X)=\ct_*(f)(X-\eta)$ gives $a_{d-1}=\ct(a_{d-1})-da_d\eta$, so $\eta=\eta_f$; this holds for $d=1$ too. The converse is the identity itself and
\cref{osq:or:thm:canonical}. $\Pi_\Lambda$ is closed under division by the nonzero ordinary scalar $da_d$.
\end{proof}

The test is a finite algebraic certificate relative to exact coefficient operations (two purely infinite parts, one translation, finitely many
comparisons), not an algorithm for arbitrary presentations of surreal numbers (18). For example, $f=\binom{X-\eta}3$ with
$\eta=\omega+\omega^{1/2}/3$ has $\ct_*(f)=\binom X3$ and $f^{-1}(\Z)=\eta+\Z$, and $\eta_f$ recovers $\eta$; whereas $\omega X^d+X$ ($d\ge2$) has
$\ct_*(f)=X$ but, its leading coefficient being infinite, at most $d$ ordinary outputs: coefficientwise reduction controls congruences, the
canonical form controls exact values (18). In the notation of \cref{osq:fs:def:centered}, $\eta_f$ is minus the purely infinite part of 16's centre
$s=a_{d-1}/(da_d)$: indeed $s=\ct(a_{d-1})/(da_d)+\mathring a_{d-1}/(da_d)$ when $a_d\in F_0$.

\begin{theorem}[At most one rich target \src{18}]\label{osq:or:thm:targets}
Let $f\in\Num_1(\Lambda)$ have degree $d\ge2$ and put $\mathcal L_{f,b}=f^{-1}(b+D)$ for $b\in\Lambda$. The following are equivalent: (i) some
$\mathcal L_{f,b}$ has more than $d$ elements; (ii) $f(X)=\ct_*(f)(X-\eta)+\beta$ for some $\eta,\beta\in\Pi_\Lambda$. Then $\eta$ and $\beta$ are
unique, $\beta=f(\eta)-\ct(f(\eta))$, and $\mathcal L_{f,b}=\eta+D$ if $\mathring b=\beta$, while $\#\mathcal L_{f,b}\le d$ otherwise.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
If $\mathcal L_{f,b}$ has more than $d$ points, \cref{osq:or:thm:canonical} applied to $f-b$ gives $f(X)-b=(\ct_*(f)-\ct(b))(X-\eta)$, which is (ii)
with $\beta=\mathring b$. Conversely (ii) and the growth of $\ct_*(f)$ give $f^{-1}(\beta+D)=\eta+D$. Since $d\ge2$, subtracting a constant does not
change $a_{d-1}$, so \cref{osq:or:thm:eta} gives the same $\eta$ for every rich target, and then $\beta=f(\eta)-\ct(f(\eta))$ because
$\ct_*(f)(0)\in D$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[One test for all targets \src{18}]\label{osq:or:cor:targettest}
For $d\ge2$ and $a_d\in F_0$, let $Q(X)=f(X+\eta_f)$. A rich target exists if and only if every nonconstant coefficient of $Q$ lies in $F_0$; then
$Q=\ct_*(f)+\beta$ with $\beta=Q(0)-\ct(Q(0))$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
This is (ii) of \cref{osq:or:thm:targets} translated back, using $\ct(\eta_f)=0$, so that $\ct_*(Q)=\ct_*(f)$.
\end{proof}

Degree one is different: for $f=aX+c$ with $0\ne a\in D$ every target $\beta+D$ has the infinite preimage $(\beta-\mathring c)/a+D$, and an infinite
linear coefficient gives at most one preimage in each target. For $(X-\omega)^2+\omega^2$ the unique rich target is $\omega^2+\Z$, with preimage
$\omega+\Z$; for $X^2$ it is $\Z$, and the target $\omega+\Z$ has exactly the two preimages $\pm\omega^{1/2}$ (18). This agrees with 16's fresh fibre
of $f(t)=\omega$ at $t=\omega^{1/2}$ (\cref{osq:fs:thm:fiber}) and with 10's bound $q_{\No,2}=2$ (\cref{osq:pm:thm:localcount}); 18 counts preimages
of whole $D$-cosets, 10 of balls at large scale, 16 of fresh values.

\begin{theorem}[Composition alignment \src{18}]\label{osq:or:thm:composition}
Let $f=p(X-\eta)+\beta$ and $g=q(X-\xi)+\gamma$ with $p,q\in\Int(D)$ of degrees $m,n\ge2$ and $\eta,\beta,\xi,\gamma\in\Pi_\Lambda$. Then $h=f\circ g$
has a target with more than $mn$ preimages if and only if $\gamma=\eta$. In that case $h(X)=(p\circ q)(X-\xi)+\beta$ and $h^{-1}(\beta+D)=\xi+D$;
otherwise $\#h^{-1}(b+D)\le mn$ for every $b\in\Lambda$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Put $\delta=\gamma-\eta$ and $Y=X-\xi$, so $h(Y+\xi)=p(q(Y)+\delta)+\beta$. If $\delta=0$ the assertions are immediate. Let $\delta\ne0$, $a=\lc(p)$ and
$c=\lc(q)$. Expanding $p(q+\delta)-p(q)=\sum_{j\ge1}p^{(j)}(q)\delta^j/j!$, every $\delta$-dependent term has degree at most $(m-1)n\le mn-2$; so the leading
coefficient $ac^m$ and the coefficient of $Y^{mn-1}$ are ordinary, and \cref{osq:or:thm:eta} gives the input translation $\xi$ for $h$. In degree
$(m-1)n\ge1$ the only $\delta$-dependent contribution is the leading term of $p'(q)\delta$, so that coefficient has purely infinite part
\[
 ma\,c^{m-1}\delta\ne0 .
\]
Since this coefficient is not constant, \cref{osq:or:cor:targettest} fails, and \cref{osq:or:thm:targets} applies to the degree-$mn$ polynomial $h$.
\end{proof}

In particular, if $f=p(X-\eta)$ and $g=q(X-\xi)$ both have infinite ordinary-output loci, $f\circ g$ has one exactly when $\eta=0$. For example
$(X-\omega)^2$ and $X^2$ both have infinite loci, but $(X^2-\omega)^2$ has the ordinary-output locus $\{\omega^{1/2},-\omega^{1/2}\}$. The hypotheses
$m,n\ge2$ are needed: a linear inner map absorbs an input translation and a linear outer map moves an output translation without creating a
nonconstant obstruction (18).

\begin{proposition}[A Gaussian simplex test \src{18}; comparison \merge]\label{osq:or:prop:gausstest}
A polynomial $F\in\SC[Z_1,\dots,Z_n]$ of total degree at most $d$ preserves $\GOz^n$ if and only if $F(\mathbf a+i\mathbf b)\in\GOz$ for all
$\mathbf a,\mathbf b\in\N^n$ with $|\mathbf a|+|\mathbf b|\le d$, a set of $\binom{2n+d}{2n}$ points. For $d\ge3$ it has fewer points than the certificate
$L_d\cup C_{d!}^{\,n}$ of \cref{osq:fs:prop:gausstest}, which contains the $(d!)^{2n}$ points of $C_{d!}^{\,n}$; for $d=1$, and for $(n,d)=(1,2)$, 16's
certificate is the smaller one. More generally, every set $S\subseteq\Z[i]$ that tests membership in $\Int(\Z[i])$ for polynomials over $\Q(i)$ of
degree at most $d$ also tests preservation of $\GOz$ for polynomials over $\SC$ of degree at most $d$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
$G(\mathbf U,\mathbf V)=F(\mathbf U+i\mathbf V)$ is a polynomial of total degree at most $d$ in $2n$ variables, so by the basis argument of
\cref{osq:fs:thm:lower} for the lower set $L_d\subseteq\N^{2n}$ its coefficients in the binomial basis $\binom{(\mathbf U,\mathbf V)}{\boldsymbol\alpha}$ are
integral combinations of its values on $L_d$, that is of $F$ at the stated points; they lie in $\GOz$, and $\binom{u}{k}\in\Oz$ for $u\in\Oz$, so
$F(\mathbf u+i\mathbf v)\in\GOz$ for $\mathbf u,\mathbf v\in\Oz^n$. Necessity is clear. For the comparison, $\binom{2n+d}{2n}<2^{2n+d}$, while
$(d!)^{2n}=36^n>8\cdot4^n=2^{2n+3}$ for $d=3$, and for $d\ge4$, $d!\ge2^d$ gives $(d!)^{2n}\ge2^{2nd}>2^{2n+d}$ since $2nd-2n-d=(2n-1)(d-1)-1\ge2$. For
$n=1$ the certificates of 16 and 18 have $2$ and $3$ points when $d=1$, and $5$ and $6$ when $d=2$. For the last assertion, interpolate $F$ at $d+1$ points of
$S$ (a testing set has at least $d+1$ points): its coefficients lie in $\Q(i)+\Pi_\C$, so $F=g+p$ with $g\in\Q(i)[X]$ and $p\in\Pi_\C[X]$, and
$g(s)=\ct(F(s))\in\Z[i]$ for $s\in S$; hence $g\in\Int(\Z[i])$ and $F$ preserves $\GOz$ by \cref{osq:fs:prop:transfer}.
\end{proof}

The comparison of the two certificates is the merge's; neither is claimed minimal. The ordinary test $\{0,\dots,d\}$ fails over $\GOz$
($\binom i2\notin\Z[i]$). By Volkov and Petrov \cite{VP}, as cited by 18, one-variable testing sets of size $O(d)$ exist for $\Int(\Z[i])$, while
$d+1$ points do not suffice for large $d$; by the last assertion they test $\GOz$ as well. These are classical inputs about $\Z[i]$, not claims of 18.

\begin{theorem}[Matrices and jets \src{18}]\label{osq:or:thm:matrix}
For $s\ge1$ let $\mathcal M_s(\Lambda)=\{f\in E[X]:f(M)\in\Lambda^{s\times s}\text{ for all }M\in\Lambda^{s\times s}\}$ (scalar coefficients) and
$\mathcal M_s(D)=\{g\in F_0[X]:g(D^{s\times s})\subseteq D^{s\times s}\}$; let $\Lambda[\varepsilon]_s=\Lambda[\varepsilon]/(\varepsilon^{s+1})$,
$\mathrm{Jet}_s(\Lambda)=\{f\in E[X]:f(\Lambda[\varepsilon]_s)\subseteq\Lambda[\varepsilon]_s\}$ and $\mathrm{Jet}_s(D)$ likewise with $F_0$ and $D$. Then
\[
 \mathcal M_s(\Lambda)=\Pi_\Lambda[X]\oplus\mathcal M_s(D),\qquad \mathrm{Jet}_s(\Lambda)=\Pi_\Lambda[X]\oplus\mathrm{Jet}_s(D),\qquad
 \bigcap_s\mathcal M_s(\Lambda)=\bigcap_s\mathrm{Jet}_s(\Lambda)=\Lambda[X].
\]
Moreover $f\in\mathrm{Jet}_s(\Lambda)$ if and only if the Hasse derivatives $f^{[r]}=\sum_{j\ge r}\binom jra_jX^{j-r}$, $r\le s$, lie in $\Num_1(\Lambda)$, and for
$\deg f\le d$ the single nilpotent Jordan block $N$ of size $d+1$ forces all coefficients into $\Lambda$, since $f(N)_{1,j+1}=a_j$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Scalar matrices give $f\in\Num_1(\Lambda)$, so $f=h+g$ with $h\in\Pi_\Lambda[X]$ and $g=\ct_*(f)\in\Int(D)$. Entrywise $\ct$ is a ring homomorphism on
matrices over $F_0+\Pi_\Lambda$ and gives $\ct(f(M))=g(\ct(M))$; so $g\in\mathcal M_s(D)$. Conversely $h(M)$ has entries in $\Pi_\Lambda$ and $g(M)$ has
entries with constant terms those of $g(\ct(M))\in D^{s\times s}$. For jets, $f(a+u)=\sum_{r\le s}f^{[r]}(a)u^r$ for $a\in\Lambda$ and nilpotent $u$ with
coefficients in $\Lambda$, and $\ct$ commutes with Hasse derivatives, which preserve $\Pi_\Lambda[X]$; apply \cref{osq:pm:thm:numreal,osq:pm:thm:numgaussian}.
The Jordan block and evaluation at $\varepsilon$ in $\Lambda[\varepsilon]_d$ read off the coefficients.
\end{proof}

For example $\binom X2$ preserves $\Oz$ but $\binom N2=-\frac12N$ for $N=\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\0&0\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)$, and
$(X^2-X)/(1+i)$ preserves $\GOz$ (as $x^2\equiv x$ modulo $1+i$ on $\Z[i]$) but has the entry $-1/(1+i)$ at $N$. These are applications of the
constant-term structure and of nilpotent evaluation; the all-size coefficient test is a general algebraic observation, not a new matrix theorem,
and the scalar-coefficient convention is one of those surveyed by Frisch \cite{Frisch} (18).

\begin{theorem}[Rational functions on discretely ordered rings \src{18}]\label{osq:or:thm:rational}
Let $B$ be a discretely ordered ring and $R_*=B$ or $B[i]$. A rational function in one variable, over an ordered field extension of $\Frac B$ or its
quadratic extension, that takes values in $R_*$ at all but finitely many points of $R_*$ where it is defined, is a polynomial and maps all of $R_*$
into $R_*$. A rational function in finitely many variables that is defined and $R_*$-valued on all of $R_*^n$ is a polynomial.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
\emph{Descent.} If $r=P/Q\in L(X)$, $\deg P\le m$, $\deg Q\le n$, takes values in a subfield $F$ at infinitely many points of $F$, choose $m+n+1$ such
nodes $t_j$ with values $v_j$; the $m+n+1$ linear equations $A(t_j)=v_jB(t_j)$ in the $m+n+2$ coefficients of $A,B$ have a nonzero solution over $F$,
$B\ne0$ (else $A$ would have too many roots), and $PB-AQ$ vanishes at the $m+n+1$ nodes, so $r=A/B\in F(X)$. \emph{Collapse.} Write $r=q+u$ with $q$
a polynomial and $u$ proper over $\Frac(R_*)$, and suppose $u\ne0$. For an ordinary $s>\deg q$, $\Delta^sr=\Delta^su$ is nonzero and proper
(\cref{osq:pm:lem:rationaldiff}); $B$ is cofinal in $\Frac B$ ($|a/b|\le|a|$), so $|\Delta^su(x)|<1$ for all sufficiently large $x\in B$. Avoiding the
finitely many exceptions, their shifts by $0,\dots,s$ and the poles, $\Delta^sr(x)=\sum_j(-1)^{s-j}\binom sjr(x+j)\in R_*$ has modulus below $1$, hence is
$0$, for infinitely many $x$, which is impossible for the nonzero $\Delta^su$. \emph{Filling.} For $a\in R_*$ choose $N$ with $a+N,\dots,a+N+\deg r$
outside the exceptions; Newton interpolation of $r(a+N+X)$ at $0,\dots,\deg r$, evaluated at $X=-N$, writes $r(a)$ as an integral combination of
allowed values. \emph{Several variables.} If $r=P/Q$ in lowest terms had a denominator of positive degree in $X_1$, a specialization of the other
variables to ordinary integers avoiding a nonzero resultant condition keeps the degrees and the coprimality, and gives a nonpolynomial one-variable
function that is $R_*$-valued on $R_*$, contrary to the first part.
\end{proof}

For $\Lambda$ this is \cref{osq:pm:thm:rational} (10), \cref{osq:fs:thm:collapse,osq:fs:thm:probes} (16), and 16's \cref{osq:fs:thm:cosmall}, which allows a
set-sized exceptional set, is stronger there; 18 adds the abstract discretely ordered ring and the finitely many exceptions. The collapse is not new in
principle: it is the D-ring phenomenon of Gunji--McQuillan \cite{GunjiMcQuillan}, as recalled by Liu \cite[Proposition~1.12]{Liu}, since a discretely
ordered ring has unit group $\{\pm1\}$ and a nonconstant polynomial cannot be unit-valued; 18's direct proof avoids importing maximal-ideal arguments
into a proper-class ring. 18 then reproves the classification of surjective rational self-maps (\cref{osq:pm:cor:bijections,osq:fs:thm:surjective}) by a
third route: a surjective $f$ has an infinite ordinary-output locus, so $f=\ct_*(f)(X-\eta)$ by \cref{osq:or:thm:canonical}, $\ct_*(f)$ is surjective on
$D$, and counting lattice points forces $\ct_*(f)=uX+c$ with $u\in D^\times$. Injectivity alone is weaker: $X^3$ is injective on $\Oz$ but not
surjective.

\begin{corollary}[Fixed Hahn workspaces \src{18}]\label{osq:or:cor:workspace}
Let $\Gamma$ be a set-sized ordered abelian group, not necessarily divisible, $J_\Gamma$ the series in $\R(\!(T^\Gamma)\!)$ with support in $\Gamma_{>0}$
(large monomials) and $A_\Gamma=\Z\oplus J_\Gamma$, with the Gaussian version $\Z[i]\oplus(J_\Gamma+iJ_\Gamma)$. Then \cref{osq:or:thm:dichotomy,osq:or:thm:canonical,osq:or:thm:eta,osq:or:thm:targets,osq:or:thm:composition,osq:or:thm:matrix,osq:or:thm:rational},
the description of the integer-valued polynomials, the finite tests and the classification of surjective rational self-maps hold for these rings.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
$A_\Gamma$ is discretely ordered (a nonzero element of $J_\Gamma$ is infinite), $\ct$ is a ring homomorphism on $\Q\oplus J_\Gamma$, and $J_\Gamma$ is closed
under ordinary scalars; the proofs use nothing else, the descent step supplying coefficients in the full Hahn field, and complex moduli being computed
in a real closure.
\end{proof}

So 18's arguments, which use only the discrete gap, survive in a fixed Hahn field, where 16's fresh-scale arguments cannot be reused
(\cref{osq:pm:sub:scope}). For $\Gamma=\{0\}$ the dichotomy is its ordinary case, and the sharp examples need an infinite element. Several variables behave
differently: $XY$ preserves $\Lambda^2$, but its ordinary-output locus contains both axes, proper classes not contained in one translate of $D^2$; what
survives is the linewise statement that $t\mapsto f(\mathbf a+t\mathbf v)$ obeys the one-variable dichotomy. The discrete gap fails in $\No$ and in the
finite surreals, and finite testing concerns polynomials of known degree only: no finite sample certifies rational functions (compare
\cref{osq:fs:thm:nosmall}), and entire Hahn series and expressions of nonstandard degree are outside the theorems (18).

\subsection{Scope of the polynomial arithmetic}\label{osq:pm:sub:scope}

Two mechanisms act in this section and have different hypotheses. The reflection theorem
\cref{osq:pm:thm:numreflection} transports the size-sensitive divisibility mechanism of
\cref{osq:thm:universal} through finite polynomial algebra; that mechanism applies to
arbitrary set coefficient fields in the class Hahn construction. The results of
\cref{osq:pm:sub:rigidity} use instead \emph{finite reduction plus lattice separation}: at
a large scale $T$, polynomial preimages of a prescribed output ball reduce to roots of
$Z^d-1$, and a nonzero reduced divided difference would force two inputs of one cluster to
lie less than $1$ apart, which the lattice forbids. This gives
\cref{osq:pm:thm:localcount,osq:pm:thm:holes} without a count of points in a surreal
interval or a real-valued asymptotic density; it uses the real or surcomplex modulus, the
standard part and the unit-spaced lattices. Neither mechanism should be used as a
substitute for the other. 16 adds a third: \emph{fresh scales} (\cref{osq:fs:sub:gaps,osq:fs:sub:tests}). A
monomial above an entire set-sized exponent group separates formal Laurent powers into disjoint support
blocks, so negative Laurent tails cannot be hidden by cancellation; this needs no lattice separation, works for
every unital $D\subseteq k$, and is a support argument, not a convergence argument in the fine topology. It needs the
full surreal exponent class: in a fixed Hahn field whose value group has no exponent above the coefficient group
it cannot be reused (16). 18 adds a fourth, \emph{finite interpolation in a discretely ordered ring}
(\cref{osq:or:sub:outputs}): the Lagrange formula for the leading coefficient, whose summands become infinitesimal when
ordinary outputs occur in two infinitely separated clusters, while a coefficient that is a rational combination of
lattice values cannot be infinitesimal. It uses neither the modulus estimates at a large scale nor fresh exponents, and
it survives in every fixed Hahn workspace (\cref{osq:or:cor:workspace}).

Manuscript~10 proposes \cref{osq:pm:thm:numreflection} and the arbitrary-scale bound
\cref{osq:pm:thm:localcount} as new; the other results of this section are explicit
consequences or transfers of classical arithmetic facts (Newton interpolation,
binomial-ring facts \cite{ElliottBinomial}, the least-common-multiple pattern \cite{CGG},
$p$-adic approximation by integer-valued polynomials \cite{CC}), proved here to make the
scope complete. The results do not classify all class ideals of $\Oz$ or of
$\Num_r(\Oz)$, all automorphisms of $\Oz$, or the factorization of arbitrary omnific
integers, and they neither construct nor use a global surcomplex exponential. They do not
assert a general transfer principle from $\Z$ to $\Oz$; the omitted type of
\cref{osq:pm:prop:type} is a concrete warning against such a principle. The Gaussian
theorem \cref{osq:pm:thm:numgaussian} does not replace the arithmetic of $\Int(\Z[i]^r)$
by a binomial basis, and no Gaussian analogue of the congruence criterion
\cref{osq:pm:thm:cp} is given. For $r\ge1$ the completion of
\cref{osq:pm:cor:completion} is a $p$-adic, not a profinite, completion. The
classification of \cref{osq:pm:cor:bijections} concerns rational functions only.
Manuscript~10 records a question suggested by this material: for the full omnific
integers, determine how much an automorphism can do inside the ideal $\Pi$ while fixing
the ordinary shadow $\ct$ (every ring endomorphism of $\Oz$ preserves $\ct$, by the
uniqueness in \cref{osq:thm:universal}). Manuscript~16 presents the image-gap, forbidden-layer and fresh-fiber
package and the two sampling statements as its proposed contributions, credits rational collapse to the D-ring
theory and Newton interpolation to the classical literature, and proves only one-variable surjectivity rigidity.
Manuscript~18 presents the ordinary-output dichotomy, the canonical translation, the uniqueness of a rich target and
composition alignment as its proposed contributions, and treats the description of $\Num_r$, the matrix and jet
reductions and the rational collapse as applications or classical material.

\section{Natural ordinal arithmetic is not a quotient of the omnific integers}\label{osq:sec:ordinal}

This section is manuscript~11's. Let $\mathcal G_{\mathrm{nat}}$ be the Grothendieck ring of the
ordinals under Hessenberg's natural sum and natural product. Every ordinal has a finite Cantor normal
form $\sum_jn_j\omega^{\gamma_j}$, each $\gamma_j$ has its own finite Cantor normal form
$\sum_\alpha m_{j,\alpha}\omega^\alpha$, and under natural multiplication
$\omega^{\gamma_j}=\prod_\alpha(\omega^{\omega^\alpha})^{m_{j,\alpha}}$. Uniqueness of these forms gives
the free commutative semiring $\N[T_\alpha:\alpha\in\On]$, and adjoining additive inverses gives
\begin{equation}\label{osq:eq:ordinalring}
 \mathcal G_{\mathrm{nat}}\cong\Z[T_\alpha:\alpha\in\On],
\end{equation}
with $T_\alpha$ represented by $\omega^{\omega^\alpha}$ under the natural embedding in $\No$. Natural
operations, not the usual noncommutative ordinal operations, are essential. The description
\eqref{osq:eq:ordinalring} appears in the question \cite{MO188430}; the argument above fixes the operations.

\begin{theorem}[The ordinal quotient question \src{11, 13}]\label{osq:thm:ordinal}
Every unital ring map $h:\Oz\to\mathcal G_{\mathrm{nat}}$ has image exactly the ordinary integral
constants. In particular $\mathcal G_{\mathrm{nat}}$ is not isomorphic to a quotient ring of $\Oz$.
More generally, no ring map $\Oz\to\Z[X]$ with $X\ne\varnothing$ is surjective.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
\emph{First proof.} $\Z[X]$ is residually set-sized by \cref{osq:thm:residual} (for any class $X$ of
indeterminates), so $h$ kills $\Pi$ and factors through $\ct$; its image is $\Z$, a proper subring
since $T_0\notin\Z$.

\emph{Second proof, specific to this target.} The additive group of $\Pi$ is divisible
($s/n\in\Pi$ for every ordinary $n\ge1$), while no nonzero element of $\Z[T_\alpha]$ is divisible by
every $n$ (each of its finitely many integral coefficients would be). So every additive map from $\Pi$
to $\Z[X]$ is zero, and a unital ring map from $\Oz=\Z+\Pi$ has image $\Z$.
\end{proof}

The question was posted by Jesse Elliott as the final addition to MathOverflow question~188430,
``Transcendence degree of the surreals over the subfield generated by the ordinals'' (30 November 2014)
\cite{MO188430}; the displayed answer, by Eric Wofsey, concerns the transcendence degree, a different
question. The answer to the quotient question is negative. 11 gives the short additive proof precisely so
that the answer is not mistaken for a difficult new factorization theorem: no claim is made that the
elementary obstruction was unknown to specialists or absent from every other source. The first proof
illustrates the general residual theorem, and the second isolates the weaker mechanism that suffices for
this target. The obstruction is not a cardinal comparison, since both rings may be proper classes; it is
their divisibility and representation properties (11).

\begin{remark}[An independent answer \src{13}]\label{osq:str:rem:elliott}
Manuscript~13, dated a day after 11 and pinned to a repository commit that precedes 11's placement, answers the same addendum negatively with
the same two proofs: its lemma that every unital map from $\Oz$ into a ring $B$ with $\bigcap_nnB=0$ factors through
$\ct$ is the second proof above, and its route through the maps
$q_F:\mathcal G_{\mathrm{nat}}\to\Z[T_\alpha:\alpha\in F]$ ($F$ finite) and the universal theorem is the first
(its corollary for rings ``separated by set-sized ring targets'' is \cref{osq:thm:residual}). 13 adds that the
inclusion $\mathcal G_{\mathrm{nat}}\subset\Oz$ has no unital ring retraction, which is immediate since every unital map
has image $\Z$, and derives \eqref{osq:eq:ordinalring} from Cantor normal forms in the same way. 13 records that the
question writes the ring as $K[\Omega]$, with $K$ a Grothendieck construction; that notation was not rechecked for
this merge. 13 does not claim to answer the original transcendence-degree question, and states that its priority as
a first answer is not established.
\end{remark}

\section{Boundaries, non-claims and open questions}\label{osq:sec:boundaries}

\subsection{What the results do not say}\label{osq:sub:notsay}
The universal theorem is a statement about homomorphisms into set-sized rings and about
set-sized modules. It is not an isomorphism $\Oz\cong\Z$; it does not classify class ideals,
class modules, automorphisms or factorizations of $\Oz$; it concerns multiplicative maps and
says nothing against additive or valuation-theoretic observations; and it fails verbatim for every
fixed set-sized exponent group, whose identity map is a set-sized target. Size alone does not prove
it (finite supports have proper-class many monomials and an augmentation), nor does divisibility
alone (only targets of positive characteristic are handled). The set-sized thresholds are exact
for the five constructions and the two fixed-group examples treated; no classification of exponent
groups or support restrictions is claimed, and generator counts follow no general law stated here.
The large-quotient results describe specified subalgebras of $\Oz/(1+\omega^a)$ and conditional
statements about primes; no existence theorem for nonarithmetic class primes is claimed.
The later additions do not change this. The flat and projective dimensions of \cref{osq:hd:sec:cores} are those of
set-sized finite-support cores, of coefficient-lattice modules and of the rings of \cref{osq:sec:homological}; they
say nothing about the homological dimensions of the class ring $\Oz$, and a flat dimension computed for one subring of
$\Oz$ bounds nothing for another (\cref{osq:hd:rem:reconcile}). The finite-support cores are not invisible to set-sized
rings, only to finite ones (\cref{osq:hd:prop:finite}). The fresh-scale theorems concern polynomials and rational
functions with finitely many coefficients and use the whole surreal exponent class. Nor do the third-batch additions:
the relation counts and $\Tor$ groups of \cref{osq:rel:sub:relations} concern the lattice ideals $J_g(M)$ and constant
matrices at one common scale, not arbitrary finitely generated ideals; the ordinary-output dichotomy is a one-variable
statement; and the fibre theorem of \cref{osq:nf:sub:fibre} gives no membership test for $\NN$. The fourth-batch addition
determines of $\Idem(\NN/\Pi\NN)$ that it is nontrivial, atomless and a proper class with independent families of every set size,
not its isomorphism type, and its descent theorem is not a decision procedure; its fixed-workspace statements concern reduced
fibres and assert neither radicality of $\Pi(G)\NN(G)$ nor atomlessness of $\Idem(\mathsf C(G))$ (\cref{osq:bb:sub:branching}).

As an internal ring, $\Oz$ has all surreal scales available through its fraction field, a nonzero
proper-class ideal $\Pi$, nonzero class-valued derivations and nonarithmetic prime quotients; as a
source of representations on sets it carries no more scalar information than $\Z$. The gap is caused
not by ignoring limits but by finite identities that become available once the source contains the
appropriate Hahn sums (06). Adapting 06's list of hypotheses that must remain visible to all the
constructions, the proofs use four different size restrictions, each for its own reason: individual supports are sets, so that a common positive gap exists; the full class of smaller
scales supplies arbitrarily large collision families; in the set-sized models an uncountable bound
admits the countable witnesses, and regularity (or cofinality, or the finite-coordinate invariant)
bounds unions of fewer than the relevant number of supports; and cardinal arithmetic enters only in
computing the size of the ring, which is the exact threshold. Dropping any of them needs a new
argument: finite supports lack the witnesses (\cref{osq:sec:support}), full supports over a fixed set
group need not have gaps (\cref{osq:rem:fstar}), and singular support bounds may preserve field closure
but not the simultaneous gap property.

\subsection{Non-claims of the sources}\label{osq:sub:nonclaims}
Every limitation stated by a source survives. They are listed and numbered by source;
several recur in the text where the corresponding result is proved.

\paragraph{03.} (1) Not peer reviewed; no Lean declarations. (2) ``Developed here'' is relative to the
inspected material; no certified priority. (3) Neither Conway's general refinement problem nor the general factorization problems for omnific
integers are settled.
(4) No classification of exponent groups or support restrictions; the cardinal theorem is a
realization theorem for its family. (5) $\cf\kappa=\aleph_0$ is excluded. (6) Only the lower bound
$\mu$ on the number of algebra generators of $\NN_\kappa$ is proved. (7) No complete integrality test
for mixed supports. (8) No descent equality $\NN\cap F^{03}_\kappa=\NN_\kappa$ (the equality holds by
\cref{osq:bb:cor:descent03}, a merge result from 20's support projection). (9) No statement on
proper-class-valued valuations. (10) Infinite set-sized images of the normalizations are not
classified, and the absence of finite images is a general consequence of containing the real
algebraic integers (19 has since classified the domain images, \cref{osq:nf:thm:domainimages}; images
with zero divisors remain unclassified). (11) No class tensor products and no class prime-ideal theorem; all $\Tor$ is over
set-sized rings. (12) The finite checks do not verify the cardinal or class arguments. (13) Restricted
Hahn fields, truncation integer parts, complete-integral-closure terminology and the small-unit lemma
are standard or elementary, with no priority claim; Kuhlmann is context only. (14) The full-class
theorem, common divisors and clearing are credited to the companion manuscript (05). (15) Density produces no convergent set-indexed net: every set-generated intermediate algebra is
uniformly discrete, so the dense normalization needs more than any set of generators. (16)
$\tfrac12\NN[i]$ is not asserted to be a ring. (17) The flatness result is an application of standard
module theory, not a new general flatness theorem. (18) Density does not impose continuity on abstract
ring homomorphisms. (19) No claim about unrestricted surcomplex analytic continuation is made.

\paragraph{04.} (1) Unrefereed; no Lean formalization; the universal theorems are candidate
contributions without certified priority. (2) No classification of all class ideals, no class Zorn
argument, no claim that proper class ideals extend to maximal ones. (3) The module equivalence covers
set-sized modules only, not class modules, group representations of $\mathrm{GL}_n(\Oz)$, or
additive homomorphisms. (4) It does not say that $P(\ct(x))=0$ implies $P(x)=0$. (5) It does not
classify infinite points of curves; the earlier project's curve questions are not declared solved.
(6) Nothing is inferred about fraction fields of the bounded-support rings. (7) The fixed-workspace
results are lower bounds only. (8) No named open conjecture is solved. (9) Additive coefficient maps
escape the theorem and are not counterexamples. (10) The telescope has a direct precursor
\cite[Remark~3.4.4]{LM}; the constant term, finite quotients and common shifts come from the prior
manuscript (02 of \cite{RepoOdg}). (11) The existence of standard part is classical and in the
repository's infrastructure. (12) The support-threshold theorem differs from fixed-group completion or
omitted-type questions. (13) The finite checks do not prove normal forms, infinite identities, class
factorizations or novelty. (14) The quotient, module, derivation and localization results are a
connected theorem package deduced from the universal theorems, not unrelated discoveries.

\paragraph{06.} (1) Unrefereed, not Lean-verified; priority not established by the targeted searches.
(2) $\pd\Z$ is shown only to be at least $2$. (3) Residue fields of nonarithmetic primes are not
classified; the prime-reservoir proposition is a necessary condition, not an existence theorem.
(4) The primality of $\omega^{\sqrt2}+\omega+1$ is imported; no factorization, refinement, GCD or
unique-factorization conjecture is used or claimed. (5) No automorphisms, class ideals or factorization
patterns are described. (6) No elementarity claim for $A^{06}_\kappa\subseteq\Oz$; no compactness
argument for a proper-class theory. (7) The Euler derivation is not asserted to be the
Berarducci--Mantova derivation or compatible with exponentiation. (8) Real closedness of
bounded-support Hahn fields is not a novelty claim, and regularity is not claimed necessary for field
closure. (9) The idea of a universal set-sized quotient is not new to the repository. (10) The module
correspondence is not a Morita equivalence of unrestricted module theories. (11) The invisible prime
quotient does not say that a particular maximal class ideal fails to exist. (12) The polynomial-test
theorem does not assert that a proper-class coordinate ring defines an ordinary scheme. (13) The
finite-congruence observation is elementary and not a strong novelty claim. (14) Numerical tests and a
successful build are not proofs; the formalization route was not executed.

\paragraph{07.} (1) Conway's refinement conjecture is neither settled nor used (the Abramov
development \cite{Abramov} is cited for its claim and caveats only); no historical priority; no referee;
no Lean. (2) The Cantor algebra is a specified subalgebra; neither the idempotents nor the prime ideals
of $\Qa_a$ are classified, and $\Qa_a$ is not claimed to be a function ring. (3) The statement on
nonarithmetic primes is conditional; no existence is asserted. (4) Surreal self-similarity is credited
to Bagayoko--van der Hoeven. (5) $\Theta_a$ is not claimed to preserve the ordinal embedding,
birthdays, simplicity or the exponential; $\vartheta$ is not additive and $\Psi$ not multiplicative.
(6) Quotient, completion and module classifications are consequences, not separate major discoveries.
(7) The small-image theorem does not say that no set-valued invariant sees positive exponents. (8) The
module equivalence is not a homological-dimension statement over class modules. (9) The global theorem
is not a theorem about small approximations of $\Oz$. (10) The finite checks do not verify the
transfinite or ambient-support arguments. (11) The CRT isomorphisms at different stages need not be
coherent; evaluation at a point of $\Z_2$ is not claimed to extend to $\Qa_a$; no topology on $\EE_a$
is used. (12) Class prime ideals are not classified, and no Zorn argument for a proper class of
ideals is used. (13) No exponential function on surcomplex arguments is assumed.

\paragraph{08.} (1) Candidate-original, not certified priority, not refereed, no Lean; the broad
factorization questions for $\Oz$ are not addressed. (2) $\Frac(A^{08}_\kappa)=F^{08}_\kappa$ is not
claimed. (3) Residue-field isomorphism types are not classified. (4) No closure under derivations or
exponentials, no initial-segment or birthday condition on the embedding. (5) The collision mechanism
has published precedents (\cite[Proposition~8.2.1]{LM}); the constant retraction and finite reductions
are not principal novelty claims. (6) Fixed Hahn workspaces lie outside the theorem (an Archimedean group has no
small scales at all). (7) The non-integral-closure corollary is not a factorization theorem. (8) Class-sized modules
are not classified; the theorem is not an isomorphism with $\Z$, and individual monomials can be
represented faithfully in small rings. (9) The Hahn derivations $D_\eta$ are not an
exponential-compatible calculus. (10) No maximal-ideal theorem for proper-class rings; no assertion that
proper class ideals lie in maximal ones. (11) The quantitative criterion gives sufficient conditions
only. (12) The finite checks prove neither the class theorem, nor summability, nor real closedness.
(13) The primality of $\omega^{\sqrt2}+\omega+1$ is an imported published theorem.

\paragraph{09.} (1) Novelty provisional after a targeted comparison; no referee; no proof-assistant
verification. (2) No named factorization conjecture solved; primality imported. (3) The number of
maximal ideals and the isomorphism types of the residue fields of $A^{09}_{\lambda,D}$ are not
determined. (4) No assertion about the unrestricted (uncountable-support) ring over
$\Gamma^{09}_\lambda$. (5) No class Zorn argument; the Jacobson statements concern set-sized rings.
(6) $\GOz$ is a subring of the surcomplex field, not the field. (7) The checks do not verify infinite
supports, cardinal arithmetic or the existence of maximal ideals. (8) The geometric identity is
elementary with precedents; profinite, derivation and flatness statements are consequences; the
Gaussian $2$-torsion computation is standard. (9) No global surcomplex exponential, sine, integration or
topology is used; the arithmetic quotient is not an ordered quotient. (10) The rotation quotient is a
conceptual predecessor, not imported. (11) No new exponential derivation is constructed and no
uniqueness of the Berarducci--Mantova derivation is claimed. (12) $I/I^2=0$ is not a
finite-presentation or smoothness claim.

\paragraph{10.} (1) Unrefereed and not machine-checked; targeted review only; no named factorization
conjecture solved. (2) For $r\ge1$ the $p$-adic completion is not a profinite completion. (3) No
classification of all class ideals, automorphisms or factorizations. (4) No transfer principle from
$\Z$ to $\Oz$. (5) $\Int(\Z[i]^r)$ is not replaced by a binomial basis. (6) The derivation $D_h$ is not the
Berarducci--Mantova derivation. (7) The universe-relative reading must carry the size restriction in
its statement. (8) The rational classification is not a classification of arbitrary functions or of
coordinatewise conjugation. (9) The congruence theorem does not make reduction modulo an infinite
omnific integer an ordinary finite congruence, and does not classify congruence-preserving class
functions. (10) Classical ingredients (Newton interpolation, binomial rings, the least-common-multiple
pattern, $p$-adic approximation) are credited, not claimed. (11) The grid test assumes exact surreal
input; no effective encoding is claimed. (12) The finite checks do not test class functions, support
lemmas or priority.

\paragraph{11.} (1) The main theorem is relative to one fixed universe, not to fragments viewed in a
larger universe. (2) No classification of prime or maximal class ideals. (3) No class module of
K\"ahler differentials. (4) The presentation theorem is not quantifier elimination or a
reconstruction of class-valued solutions. (5) Sharpness of the coinitiality theorem is stated under
$\kappa^{<\kappa}=\kappa$ and $|K|\le\kappa$ as explicit extra assumptions (the unconditional value is a
merge result, \cref{osq:prop:card11}). (6) Necessary closure conditions for intermediate rings are not
given. (7) The ordinal answer is not claimed hard or unknown to specialists. (8) Bounded-support
coefficient rings and lower-scale geometric division have precedents in \cite[Section~8]{LM}.
(9) Targeted novelty assessment only; the finite checks prove no infinite-support or class-size
statement. (10) The rotation-group theme is acknowledged, not originated. (11) The only answer to
the MathOverflow question concerns transcendence degree. (12) No primality or refinement conjecture is
newly settled; the ideals, class-valued representations and factorization theory of quotients invisible
to set-sized rings are not determined, and ordinary factorization questions about $\Oz$ remain
outside the conclusions.
